Kinematic flow from the flow of cuts
Ross Glew 1 and, Andrzej Pokraka 2

Abstract
The wavefunction coefficients of conformally coupled scalars in power-law FRW cosmologies satisfy differential equations governed by a set of simple combinatorial rules known as the kinematic flow . In this paper we derive the kinematic flow, expressed using a set of differential forms referred to as the cut basis , from a geometric perspective, relying solely on the cosmological hyperplane arrangement and without invoking bulk physics. Each element of the cut basis corresponds to the positive geometry associated to an independent cut of the physical FRW-form and can be labeled by decorating (minors of) the truncated Feynman graph with an acyclic orientation. We provide a straightforward prescription to associate a logarithmic differential form to each element of the cut basis by considering its corresponding decorated graph. Moreover, we show that the residues of the physical FRW-form are canonical forms of certain graphical zonotopes labeled by the same set of decorated graphs. These zonotopes control the cut combinatorics— flow of cuts —of the physical FRW-form and the cut basis (by construction). Using the theory of relative twisted cohomology and intersection theory , we derive a closed form formula for the differential equations of the cut basis. We also introduce combinatorial rules that compute the kinematic differential of any basis element without explicit calculation. The combinatorics of our differential equations is a natural consequence of the flow of cuts and is equivalent (up to rescaling) to the kinematic flow for the recently studied time integral basis. In particular, our differential equations decouple into exponentially many sectors, one for each way of cutting a subset of edges of the graph.



1
Introduction

In recent years, significant advances have been made in the study of scattering amplitudes driven by their connection to geometry. From the geometric point of view, certain scattering amplitudes are represented as canonical differential forms associated with positive geometries defined directly in the kinematic space of the underlying theory
Arkani-Hamed:2017tmz
. Traditional properties of scattering amplitudes, such as factorization, then emerge naturally from the boundary structure of these geometries. The first example of this phenomenon was the amplituhedron whose associated canonical form calculates the tree-level scattering amplitudes and all loop integrand of planar
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
Arkani-Hamed:2013jha
. Since then similar discoveries have been made for scattering amplitudes in other theories most notably
\text{tr}(\phi^{3})
and ABJM
Arkani-Hamed:2017mur
;
He:2023rou
. An exciting question moving forward is whether the techniques developed for the study of scattering amplitudes can be applied more generally to other physical quantities.

One such family of observables receiving increased attention by the amplitudes community is that of
cosmological correlation functions
. Just as the cross section in particle physics is derived from the more fundamental scattering amplitudes, these cosmological observables are also derived from a more fundamental object, poetically named the
wavefunction of the universe
. An initial connection between the wavefunction and geometry was made by the discovery of the cosmological polytope, the canonical form of which computes a contribution to the flat space wavefunction coefficients arising from a single Feynman graph
Arkani-Hamed:2017fdk
.

Importantly, the flat space wavefunction coefficients serve as the fundamental ingredient in the computation of wavefunction coefficients in cosmologies beyond flat spacetime. The most commonly studied toy model, also the focus of this work, consists of conformally coupled scalars with polynomial interactions in a Friedmann-Robertson-Walker (FRW) cosmology. In this broader context, the wavefunction coefficients are no longer simple rational functions, but rather complicated analytic functions represented as certain twisted integrals; the behavior of which is governed by the theory of twisted cohomology. While the integrand of the wavefunction coefficient defines only one element of the twisted cohomology, it plays a special role in physics and our story.
Therefore, we will refer to this distinguished element of the twisted cohomology as the
physical FRW-form
.

Moreover, since the theory of twisted cohomology is a finite dimensional vector space, as is familiar from the study of loop amplitudes
Henn:2013pwa
,
it follows that the basis of twisted integrals satisfy coupled linear differential equations written schematically as

Here,

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern is an appropriate choice of basis for the twisted cohomology into which the contribution to the wavefunction coefficient associated to a given graph can be expanded,
\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}
is the exterior derivative with respect to the kinematic variables and
{\bf A}_{G}
is the connection matrix encoding the differential equations.
A particular basis was first explored in
Arkani-Hamed:2023bsv
;
Arkani-Hamed:2023kig
, where it was observed that the resulting differential equations obey a set of simple combinatorial rules known as the
kinematic flow
, expressed in terms of graph tubings. The kinematic flow has since been studied in detail in
Arkani-Hamed:2023bsv
;
Baumann:2024mvm
;
Hang:2024xas
;
De:2024zic
;
He:2024olr
;
Baumann:2025qjx
;
Capuano:2025ehm
.

In this paper, we refine our understanding of the
cut basis
proposed in
De:2024zic
and provide a purely geometric derivation of kinematic flow.
1
1

1

Mathematically, cutting a propagator (denominator of a rational function) should be understood as taking the residue on the vanishing loci of that denominator. The jargon “cut” and “propagator” come from the specific physical significance of these denominators and residues.

The cut basis is intimately tied to the structure of the untwisted hyperplane arrangement of the propagators and the associated positive geometry on each maximal cut
2
2

2

The maximal cut of an integral, is one where every propagator (special denominator) is cut.

of the physical FRW integral.
It is constructed by leveraging a combinatorial understanding of the non-trivial sequential cuts/residues of the physical FRW integrand (flow of cuts) to isolate the independent cuts of the physical FRW form.
This construction guarantees that the cut basis is a basis for the
minimal
subspace of integrals that couple to the physical FRW integral in the differential equations.
Specifically, the physical sequential residue operators—those that do not annihilate the physical integrand—can be used to project onto the physical subspace providing an invariant definition for the subspace of interest
De:2024zic
.
3
3

3

In mathematics, such a subspace is called a monodromy invariant subspace.
This is because the discontinuities (related to monodromies) of the physical FRW integral have integral representations where one replaces the physical integration contour with a contour that has a sequential residue as a component.

,
4
4

4

In physics, using sequential residues to project integrals onto one another is called generalized unitarity.
However, there are many situations where the sequential residue does not fully localize the integral.
Intersection theory provides an algorithmic way to construct residues operators that localize any remaining integration and project one integral onto another.

Like the kinematic flow, differential equations for the cut basis can be written down without explicit calculation by considering the combinatorics of certain decorated graphs; we derive this combinatorial formula using simple residue calculus originating from intersection theory.

It is important to note that the time integral basis used in the most recent formulation of the kinematics flow is equivalent to the cut basis up to a trivial rescaling after performing integration-by-parts
He:2024olr
;
Baumann:2025qjx
. However, it suffers from two disadvantages, neither of which are present in our construction. First, as its name suggests, the construction of the time integral basis relies on bulk physics. This contradicts the original intent of the kinematic flow and positive geometry program: to formulate physics in such a way that bulk physics is emergent rather than an input.
Second, many of the integrals in
Baumann:2025qjx
have integrands that appear with higher-order poles.
While these are integration-by-parts equivalent to integrands with simple poles, this extra integration-by-parts step obscures the connection to the underlying positive geometry.

Summary of results

In the remainder of the introduction we summarize the main results of the paper, including our choice of basis and its combinatorial interpretation, as well as the structure of the resulting differential equations and their connection to the geometry of zonotopes.

Cut basis from positive geometries.

Of central importance to our analysis is the cosmological hyperplane arrangement,
\mathcal{B}
, a collection of hyperplanes labeled by connected subgraphs (also known as tubes) of the truncated Feynman graph
G
. In addition to these, the coordinate hyperplanes,
\mathcal{T}
, play a distinguished role as twisted planes.
We will often refer to the cosmological hyperplane arrangement,
\mathcal{B}
, as the untwisted hyperplane arrangement and the coordinate hyperplanes,
\mathcal{T}
, as the twisted hyperplane arrangement.
For example, in the case of the three-site chain,
G=\includegraphics[align]{figures/p3.pdf}
, the arrangement consists of nine planes: the three (twisted) coordinate hyperplanes and six (untwisted) planes corresponding to the tubes.
Each untwisted hyperplane is the vanishing loci of a polynomial labeled by
B_{\bullet}
:

(17)

The basis of integrals used in this paper is motivated by the combinatorics of the cuts of the physical FRW-form presented in
De:2024zic
.
There, it was shown that the set of (physical) cuts
can be indexed by a combinatorial construction on the graph referred to as
cut-tubings
.
A cut tubing,
\tau
, is a set of tubes which satisfy compatibility conditions (see
De:2024zic
for details).
Each cut tubing,
\tau
, indexes the intersection
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}:=\cap_{t\in\tau}\mathsf{V}(B_{t})\subset\mathcal{B}
5
5

5

We use
\mathsf{V}
for the vanishing symbol to distinguish it from
V_{G}
, which is used for the set of vertices of a graph
G
.
Given a polynomial
P
,
\mathsf{V}(P)
is the variety corresponding to the vanishing loci of
P
.

such that, given a global ordering for the
B_{\bullet}
, the sequential residue is compatible with this ordering and
does not
annihilate the physical FRW integrand. The cut tubings for the three-site chain are shown in figure
1
.

Figure 1:
The cut tubings for the path graph on three vertices. The two cut tubings highlighted in blue are degenerate.

As argued in
De:2024zic
, restricting the twisted arrangement
\mathcal{T}
(coordinate hyperplanes) to the intersection of untwisted planes labeled by a cut tubing (i.e.,
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
), defines a single bounded chamber.
This bounded chamber is an example of a
positive geometry
\Gamma_{\tau}

Arkani-Hamed:2017tmz
, and, as such, comes equipped with a natural logarithmic differential form
\Omega_{\Gamma_{\tau}}
called the canonical form.
Each element of the cut basis is a differential form generated from two pieces of information:
the positive geometry of a cut and the cut istelf.
Schematically, every element of the cut basis can be bought to the form
\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau}\propto\bigwedge_{t\in\tau}\mathrm{dlog}B_{t}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}_{\Gamma_{\tau}}
such that
\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau}
becomes the canonical form of
{\Gamma_{\tau}}
after cutting all
B_{t\in\tau}
(i.e., taking the maximal cut of
\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau}
)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Gamma_{\tau}}=\text{Res}_{B_{t_{|\tau|}}=0}{\circ}{\cdots}{\circ}\text{Res}_{B_{t_{1}}=0}[\mathrm{dlog}B_{t_{1}}{\wedge}{\cdots}{\wedge}\mathrm{dlog}B_{t_{|\tau|}}{\wedge}\tilde{\Omega}_{\Gamma\tau}]{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}=\tilde{\Omega}_{\Gamma_{\tau}}|_{B_{t\in\tau}=0}}\,.

(18)

The positive geometry
{\Gamma_{\tau}}
is extremely simple: it is the cartesian product of simplicies.
This simplifies the construction of
\tilde{\Omega}_{\Gamma_{\tau}}
and the residue calculus from intersection theory.
Moreover, each element of the cut basis can be conveniently packaged into a simple one-line combinatorial formula presented in the main text.

Figure 2:
The acyclic minors for the path graph on three vertices. The acyclic minor highlighted in blue is associated to two degenerate cut tubings.

Resolving degeneracies.

One disadvantage of working with the set of cut tubings is that they are generally
degenerate
, in the sense that multiple cut tubings can label the same geometric space
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}=\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.
This stems from linear dependencies between the hyperplanes and leads to linear relations among the forms
\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau}
.
For example, the cut tubings highlighted in blue on figure
1
are degenerate and label the same cut

A solution this degeneracy problem is to work instead with the set of
acyclic minors
of the graph
Glew:2025arc
;
Glew:2025ugf
. An acyclic minor
\mathfrak{g}
is a graph formed by first deleting and/or contracting a subset of edges of
G
, then assigning an acyclic orientation to the remaining edges. Remarkably, the set of acyclic minors counts each degenerate family of cut tubings exactly once! Building on this observation, it is natural to assign a differential form directly to the acyclic minor given by

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau},

(20)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
denotes the set of degenerate cuts associated to the acyclic minor
\mathfrak{g}
. We refer to the set of forms generated by the acyclic minors
\{\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\}
as the cut basis (these match the definitions in
De:2024zic
up to signs).

Returning to the example of the three-site chain, the complete set of acyclic minors are shown in figure
2
. The oriented graph highlighted in
blue
is associated to the two highlighted cut tubings in figure
1
. Therefore, the form associated to this degenerate cut is

(21)

The remaining (black) diagrams in figures
1
and
2
are in one-to-one correspondence and have been ordered accordingly.

Emergence of zonotopes.

The physical FRW-form is represented in terms of the cut basis as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t1}}{=}&(\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t2}}{+}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t3}}{+}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t4}}{+}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t5}}){-}(\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t8}}{+}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t9}}){-}(\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t6}}{+}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t7}}){+}(\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t10}})\,.\end{aligned}

(22)

Each term in this expression can be translated to a distinct point
{\bf x}^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathcal{B}_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
in the cosmological hyperplane arrangement. Remarkably, taking the convex hull of the points grouped as above yields a family of graphical zonotopes

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cc.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t2}},{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t3}},{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t4}},{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t5}}\},

(23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cb.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t8}},{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t9}}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bc.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t6}},{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t7}}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bb.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t10}}\}.

As we will see, the combinatorics of compatible cuts—referred to as the
flow of cuts
—is encoded in these zonotopes. This can be seen by noting that the residues of the physical FRW-form, evaluated on any collection of tubes that partition the vertices of the graph, coincide with the canonical form of a zonotope or one of its boundaries

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_cut_1}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cc.pdf}}]\,,&\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_cut_2}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cb.pdf}}]\,,\\
\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_cut_3}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bc.pdf}}]\,,&\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_cut_4}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bb.pdf}}]\,.\end{aligned}

(24)

Further sequential residues of the physical FRW-form are governed by the combinatorics of these zonotopes, giving rise to the flow of cuts illustrated in figure
3
.

Figure 3:
Flow of cuts for the 3-site chain graph.
The top-dimensional facets of the zonotope are
red
, the codimension-1 facets are
cyan
and the codimension-2 facets are
magenta
.

Kinematic flow.

It turns out that the kinematic flow inherits the reverse flow structure defined by the flow of cuts. As a result, the differential equations decouple into exponentially many sectors, one for each subset of edges in the graph. Within a given block, a function
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
couples to another
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}
if and only if the zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}
forms a codimension-one boundary of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. For instance, the differential equations for the path graph decompose into four blocks. Consider the zonotope represented by the line segment at the top left of figure
3
, the corresponding differential equations are given explicitly by

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}},

(25)

where the graphs highlighted in red correspond to
\mathrm{dlog}
’s whose arguments are functions of the graph variables referred to as
letters
. These same phenomena were recently observed in
Baumann:2025qjx
, albeit from a more physics-inspired approach, through the study of the time integral representation of the wavefunction.
In contrast, our approach reveals the geometric origin of zonotopes in the kinematic flow, without invoking any knowledge of bulk physics.

Outline

In Section
2
we introduce the central object of interest: the wavefunction of the universe.
This will include the combinatorial interpretation of the wavefunction in terms of tubings on graphs and a minimal discussion on twisted cohomology/intersection theory relevant for computing the differential equations.
Next, in section
3
, we describe how to construct the cut basis and deal with the degeneracy of the cosmological hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{B}
.
We also introduce important combinatorial objects, called letters, that characterize the geometry and combinatorics of the cuts as well as the singularities that enter the differential equations.
After providing a simple combinatorial formula for the elements of the cut basis, we connect these forms to a positive geometry on the cut.
In section
4
we show how the residues of the physical FRW-form are canonical forms of a certain family of zonotopes.
From this, we conclude that the combinatorics of the sequential residues of the physical FRW-form is governed by the geometry of certain zonotopes which we call the “flow of cuts”.
Finally, in section
5
, we derive the kinematic flow rules from the residue calculus of intersection theory.
In this derivation, the flow of cuts provides important selection rules that control the combinatorics/organization of the differential equations.
In fact, the kinematic flow is simply the cut flow in reverse.
The remainder of section
5
is dedicated to examples.

2
Cosmological wavefunction

In this work, we consider a toy model for quantum cosmology: conformally-coupled scalars in a power-law FRW cosmology with non-conformal polynomial interactions.
This model is characterized by the following action in a
(d+1)
-dimensional space-time

\displaystyle S=\int\text{d}^{d}x\;\text{d}\eta\;\sqrt{-g}\left[-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{d-1}{8d}R\phi^{2}-\sum_{p\geq 3}\frac{\lambda_{p}}{p!}\phi^{p}\right].

(26)

Here, the invariant line element of the FRW spacetime in comoving coordinates with conformal time
\eta\in(-\infty,0]
is
\text{d}s^{2}=a^{2}(\eta)\left(-\text{d}\eta^{2}+\text{d}x_{i}\text{d}x^{i}\right)
where the index
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
runs over spatial dimensions.
Furthermore, we assume that the scale factor is a power law
a(\eta)=(\eta/\eta_{0})^{-(1+\epsilon)}
, with constant parameters
\eta_{0}
and
\epsilon
.
Depending on the choice of the cosmological parameter
\epsilon
many cosmologies of interest can be recovered including: inflationary (
\epsilon\approx 0
), de Sitter (
\epsilon=0
) and flat-space (
\epsilon=-1
) for example.

In a cosmological setting, a key set of physical observables are the
cosmological correlation functions
at fixed conformal time
\eta=0
. Much like expectation values in standard quantum mechanics, cosmological correlators are computed via the Born rule

\displaystyle\langle\Phi({\bf x_{1}}){\cdots}\Phi({\bf x_{n}})\rangle=\int\mathcal{D}\Phi\ \Phi({\bf x_{1}})\cdots\Phi({\bf x_{n}})|\Psi[\Phi]|^{2},

(27)

where
\Phi({\bf x})=\phi({\bf x},0)
is the boundary configuration of the field
\phi({\bf x},\eta)
and the squared modulus of the
wavefunction of the universe

|\Psi[\Phi]|^{2}
is a probability distribution over the space of field configurations. The wavefunction
\Psi[\Phi]
is defined formally as the path integral over all field configurations
\phi({\bf x},\eta)
interpolating between the Bunch-Davies vacuum in the far past,
\eta=-\infty(1-i\epsilon)
, and a specified field configuration
\phi({\bf x},0)=\Phi({\bf x})
at the boundary.
Importantly, the wavefunction admits an expansion in Fourier space

\displaystyle\Psi[\Phi]{=}\!\exp\!\!\left[\sum_{n}\frac{i}{n!}{\int}\frac{d^{d}{\bf k}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{d}}{\cdots}\frac{d^{d}{\bf k}_{n}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Phi({\bf k}_{1}){\cdots}\Phi({\bf k}_{n})\psi^{(n)}(\{{\bf k}_{a}\}_{a{=}1}^{n})(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}\!({\bf k}_{1}{+}{\ldots}{+}{\bf k}_{n})\right]\!\!,

(28)

where the integration kernels
\psi^{(n)}
are the so called
wavefunction coefficients
that depend on the
n
spatial momenta
\{{\bf k}_{i}\}
associated to field insertions on the late-time boundary (
\eta=0
). The magnitudes of the spatial momenta
|{\bf k}_{i}|
are commonly referred to as
energies
.

These wavefunction coefficients can be computed perturbatively as a sum over Feynman graphs; tree-level and one-loop examples are displayed in figure
4
.
For a given Feynman graph, the contribution to the wavefunction coefficient depends on the total external energies entering each vertex, represented by
X_{v}
, and the internal energies flowing through each propagator, denoted by
Y_{e}
.
As such it is useful to work at the level of
truncated
graphs, obtained by removing all external (gray on figure
4
) legs from the original Feynman graph.
However, as reviewed in the next section, the full Feynman rules are not needed to compute the contribution,
\psi_{G}
, to the wavefunction coefficient from a given truncated graph
G
.
6
6

6

Readers interested in the additional details may consult
Baumann:2025qjx
.

Importantly, the FRW wavefunction coefficients
\psi_{G}
can be constructed by shifting their flat-space counterparts
\hat{\Omega}_{G}
and integrating against a specific kernel.
Therefore, we begin by reviewing some key features of the flat-space wavefunction coefficients.

Figure 4:
Examples of Feynman diagrams contributing to the wavefunction coefficient at tree-level and one-loop.

2.1
The flat-space wavefunction

For a given graph
G
the cosmological polytope
\mathcal{P}_{G}
is a positive geometry whose canonical differential form
\Omega_{G}
encodes the corresponding contribution to the flat-space wavefunction coefficient
Arkani-Hamed:2017fdk
. In this section, we review how to compute the canonical form of the cosmological polytope by considering the nested structure of subgraphs of
G
.

Given a graph
G=(V_{G},E_{G})
with vertex set
V_{G}
and edge set
E_{G}
, we refer to the set of all
connected subgraphs
as
tubes
. In explicit examples we will represent a tube graphically on
G
by encircling its corresponding subgraph. A tube
t=(V_{t},E_{t})
is said to cross an edge
v\overset{e}{\text{---}}v^{\prime}\in E_{G}
if
e\not\in E_{t}
and
V_{t}\cap\{v,v^{\prime}\}\neq\emptyset
. We say two tubes are
compatible
if one is a subgraph of the other or they do not intersect on any vertices. We refer to a
k
-element set of pairwise compatible tubes as a
k
-tubing. A tubing is
maximal
if no more compatible tubes can be added.
7
7

7

Specifically, each maximal tubing contains exactly
|V_{G}|+|E_{G}|
many tubes.

We denote the set of all tubes of a graph by
T_{G}

and the set of all maximal tubings by
\mathcal{T}^{\text{max}}_{G}
.

The canonical form of the cosmological polytope
\Omega_{G}
, equivalently the flat-space wavefunction coefficient
\hat{\Omega}_{G}
, has a simple expression in terms of maximal tubings

\displaystyle\Omega_{G}

\displaystyle=\hat{\Omega}_{G}({\bf X},{\bf Y})\frac{\text{d}^{|V_{G}|}{\bf X}\wedge\text{d}^{|E_{G}|}{\bf Y}}{\mathrm{GL}(1)},

\displaystyle\hat{\Omega}_{G}({\bf X},{\bf Y})=\prod_{e\in E_{G}}2Y_{e}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{G}^{\text{max}}}\frac{1}{S_{\tau}}.

(29)

Here,
S_{\tau}=\prod_{t\in\tau}S_{t}
and the
S_{t}
are linear functions (“propagators”) associated to each tube

\displaystyle S_{t}=\sum_{v\in V_{t}}X_{v}+\sum_{e\text{ cross }t}Y_{e},

(30)

where the first sum is over all vertices contained in the tube and the second sum is over all edges crossed by the tube counted with multiplicity.
Kinematic variables
{\bf X}=\{X_{v}:v\in V_{G}\}
and
{\bf Y}=\{Y_{e}:e\in E_{G}\}
are assigned to each vertex and edge of the graph;
X_{v}
is the sum over all external energies,
|\mathbf{k}_{\bullet}|
, connected to the vertex
v
while
Y_{e}
is the energy of the exchanged particle of edge
e
.

To understand these definitions, consider the flat-space wavefunction coefficient for the three-site chain, with variables assigned as in figure
4
.
Using (
29
) and noting that there are two maximal tubings, one finds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hat{\Omega}_{\hbox to25.64pt{\vbox to2.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.44pt\lower-1.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(32)

4
right) is

\displaystyle S_{\hbox to24.7pt{\vbox to16.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.8143pt\lower-26.79225pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(34)

2.2
From flat-space to cosmology

The flat-space wavefunction coefficients can be recycled to produce FRW wavefunction coefficients; they act as a universal integrand from which the corresponding wavefunctions in any power-law FRW space-time can be computed. To obtain the cosmological wavefunction from its flat-space counterpart we simply shift the kinematic variables associated to the graphs vertices and integrate against the so called
twist
factor
u
:

\displaystyle\psi_{G}=\int_{0}^{\infty}u\Psi_{G},\ \ \text{ where }\ \ \Psi_{G}=\hat{\Omega}_{G}({\bf x}+{\bf X},{\bf Y})\ \text{d}^{|V_{G}|}{\bf x}.

(35)

Here, the twist is defined as
u=\prod_{v\in V_{G}}x_{v}^{\alpha_{v}}

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{v}=d+\epsilon(d+1)+\frac{1}{2}p_{v}(1+\epsilon)(1-d)\,,

(36)

d
is the number of spatial dimensions
d
, and
p_{v}
is the valency of the vertex
v
.
It is a multi-valued power function with
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
(since
\epsilon\notin\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
) that vanishes on the coordinate hyperplanes
x_{v}=0
. Due to the twist
u
, the coordinate hyperplanes are branch surfaces of the integrand in (
35
).
We call the coordinate hyperplanes
\mathcal{T}_{i}:=\mathsf{V}(x_{i})
twisted hyperplanes
and define the variety
\mathcal{T}=\mathsf{V}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})
, which encodes the singular loci of the integrand in (
35
) associated to the twist
u
.

The integrand in (
35
) also inherits singularities from the the shifted flat-space wavefunction coefficient
\hat{\Omega}_{G}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})
.
The shifted flat-space canonical function is obtained by replacing all
S_{t}
in (
29
) by

\displaystyle B_{t}=\sum_{v\in t}x_{v}+S_{t}\,.

(37)

The integrand in (
35
) has potential singularities on the vanishing loci of the
B_{t}
:
\mathcal{B}:=\mathsf{V}\left(\prod_{t}B_{t}\right)
.
We call the hyperplanes
\mathcal{B}_{t}:=\mathsf{V}(B_{t})
untwisted as they are not components of
\mathcal{T}
.
The nature of singularities associated to
\mathcal{B}
are very different from those associated to
\mathcal{T}
:
\mathcal{B}
is the loci where
u\Psi_{G}
has potential poles (not branch cuts).
In more traditional quantum field theory language, the
B_{i}
’s should be thought of as propagators.
Due to the distinct nature of the singularities associated to
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we will have to treat these varieties differently when constructing the relevant cohomology theory in the next section.

2.3
Structural overview of FRW-forms

In this section, we provide an intuitive overview of the relevant aspects of twisted cohomology and intersection theory that motivates the cut-first perspective of our basis.

FRW-cohomology.

Due to the presence of the twist
u
in (
35
), the cohomology theory describing such integrals is a twisted cohomology.
Moreover, since the twist
u
only regulates the twisted singular loci of
u\Psi_{G}
(
\mathcal{T}
), the cohomology associated to (
35
) can be thought of as a specialization of the twisted cohomology where the
B_{t}
are included in the twist
u_{\text{deformed}}:=\prod_{i}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\prod_{t}B_{t}^{\beta_{i}}
by setting
\beta_{t}=0
:
u=u_{\text{deformed}}|_{\beta_{\bullet}=0}
.
Explicitly, that is

\displaystyle H^{n}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla):=\frac{\{\text{$\nabla$-closed $n$-forms}\}}{\{\text{$\nabla$-exact $n$-forms}\}},

(38)

where
M:=\mathbb{C}^{|V_{G}|}\setminus\mathcal{T}
,
\nabla:=\text{d}+\omega\wedge
, and
\omega:=\mathrm{dlog}u
a flat connection.
8
8

8

A differential
n
-form
\varphi
is
\nabla
-closed
if
\nabla\varphi=0
and
\nabla
-exact
if
\varphi=\nabla\psi
for some (
n-1
)-form
\psi
.

This is simply the de Rham cohomology of
M\setminus\mathcal{B}
with respect to the covariant derivative
\nabla
.

While the above specialization changes the mathematical structure of the usual twisted cohomology (most notably in the dual cohomology introduced below), it preserves most of its key properties.
In particular, the existence of a middle dimensional theorem:
9
9

9

Note that the middle dimension theorem only holds when
\mathcal{T}
satisfies certain generality assumptions that have been omitted.

10
10

10

Top-dimensional holomorphic forms are middle dimensional since the real-dimension of a complex manifold or variety is twice that of the complex-dimension.

\displaystyle\text{dim}H^{n}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla)=\begin{cases}|\chi(M\setminus\mathcal{B})|&n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(M\setminus\mathcal{B})=|V_{G}|\,,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

(39)

where
\chi
is the signed Euler characteristic of the variety
M\setminus\mathcal{B}
(or hyperplane arrangement).
This means that we expect the basis of cosmological integrals for a given graph to have size
|\chi(M\setminus\mathcal{B})|
.
In general, all FRW-forms can be expressed as a top-dimensional holomorphic form

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}^{|V_{G}|}\mathbf{x}}{x_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\mu_{n}}B_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\cdots B_{m}^{\nu_{m}}},

(40)

where
\mu_{i},\nu_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that all top-dimensional holomorphic forms are trivially
\nabla
-closed; one only needs to check exactness when building a basis for the FRW-cohomology.

A natural organization for the FRW-cohomology is by the sets of propagators that appear in the denominator.
Schematically, each basis element can be expressed as

\displaystyle\underset{\text{can be cut}}{\underbrace{\left(\bigwedge_{t\in T}\mathrm{dlog}B_{t}\right)}}\wedge\underset{\text{positive geometry}}{\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i}\pm\bigwedge_{j\in J_{i}}\mathrm{dlog}x_{j}\right)}}\,,

(41)

where
T
and the
J_{i}
are multi-indices indexing which untwisted and twisted divisors appear such that
|T|+|J_{i}|=|V_{G}|\;\forall\;i
.
The first factor contains only singularities in
\mathcal{B}

\mathrm{dlog}
’s (or possibly a linear combination of such forms) on which we can take generalized unitarity cuts.
Moreover, when building our basis, we require the first factor to be linearly independent from from all other propagator
\mathrm{dlog}
-forms in the basis.
This condition is non-trivial for the degenerate arrangements seen in cosmology (see section
3.1
).
The second factor is a linear combination of
\mathrm{dlog}
’s with poles on the twisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{T}
.
The exact linear combinations that appear come from positive geometric considerations (sections
3.3
and
3.4
).

Residues and (generalized unitarity) cuts

Central to our analysis is the idea of a (generalized unitarity) cut.
Despite the fancy name, a cut is equivalent to taking the residue on the vanishing loci of a collection of
B_{t}
’s.
11
11

11

These residues are linked to discontinuities which, in turn, are related to the notion of (generalized) unitarity in quantum field theory.

For example, cutting the “propagators”
B_{t_{1}},B_{t_{2}},\dots,B_{t_{|T|}}
corresponds to the sequential residue
\text{Res}_{T}:=\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{j_{|T|}}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{t_{1}}}
.

Importantly, the FRW-cohomology can be built from the twisted cohomology of
simpler
cut spaces
M_{T}
that are reached by taking sequential residues:

\displaystyle M_{T}

\displaystyle:=M\cap\mathcal{B}_{T}\,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{T}

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{t\in T}\mathsf{V}(B_{t})\,.

(42)

The
M_{T}
’s are positive geometries, and the (generalized unitarity) cuts
\text{Res}_{T}
are induced by the dual forms (detailed below) in the intersection number.

Additionally, the
physical cuts
—those that do not annihilate the physical FRW-form
\Psi_{G}
—serve as projectors onto a distinguished subspace of the full FRW-cohomology called the
physical subspace
.
The physical subspace contains the physical FRW-form
\Psi_{G}
such that the differential equations for any chosen basis close.
Moreover, the dimension of the physical subspace is generically much less than
|\chi(M\setminus\mathcal{B})|
—the dimension of the full FRW-cohomology.
This was first observed in
Arkani-Hamed:2023kig
and then it was understood how to isolate this subspace using physical sequential residues in
De:2024zic
.

The dual cohomology.

The FRW twisted cohomology is a vector space equipped with an inner product called the
intersection number
.
Like all inner products, the intersection number is a pairing between a vector space
H^{n}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla)
and the associated dual space
\check{H}^{|V_{G}|}
(to be discussed)

\displaystyle\check{H}^{|V_{G}|}\times H^{|V_{G}|}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla)\to\mathbb{C}\quad\text{via}\quad\langle\check{\varphi}|\phi\rangle:=\text{Res}_{\check{\varphi}}[\phi]\,.

(43)

Here,
\check{\varphi}
is a dual form in the dual vector space and
\phi\in H^{|V_{G}|}(M\setminus\mathcal{B};\nabla)
is any FRW-form.
For our purposes, the intersection number is equivalent to the action of a specific residue operator
\text{Res}_{\check{\varphi}}
generated by the dual form
\check{\varphi}
.
This residue operator includes the residues associated to the cuts discussed above as well as additional residue operators that fully localize the FRW-form
\phi
.
The intersection number is an algebraic operation; no integration is required—an improvement over generalized unitarity which still requires one to compute discontinuities via integration.
Besides entering as a component of the inner product, the dual cohomology suggests a natural organizational structure for the FRW-cohomology based on cuts (c.f., (
41
) and (
58
)).
After constructing the relevant dual space, we will use the intersection number to derive a closed form formula for the differential equations without relying on input from bulk physics.

The dual space is a relative twisted cohomology where the variety
\mathcal{B}
acts as boundaries instead of the loci for possible poles:

\check{H}^{|V_{G}|}:=H^{|V_{G}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\nabla})
.
The swapping of poles and boundaries is needed to make the definition of the intersection number finite and well defined.
12
12

12

In order to construct the residue operator
\text{Res}_{\check{\varphi}}
from
\check{\varphi}
, one needs to be able to integrate
\check{\varphi}
infinitesimally near the components of
\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{T}
using only rational functions.
This is not possible for any
\check{\varphi}
that has a simple pole on a component of
\mathcal{B}
; a logarithm is needed to integrate an untwisted pole.
Therefore, we simply have to enforce that all dual forms are regular on all components of
\mathcal{B}
; i.e., that the dual space is a relative cohomology with boundaries
\mathcal{B}
.
The technical details can be found in
Caron-Huot:2021xqj
;
Caron-Huot:2021iev
.

Moreover, this requires dual forms to be regular on all untwisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{B}_{t}
; poles on the twisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{T}
are allowed.
Dual integrals also come with the inverse twist
\check{u}=u^{-1}
.
Hence, the relative twisted cohomology is equipped with the covariant derivative
\check{\nabla}:=\text{d}+\check{\omega}\wedge
where
\check{\omega}=-\omega
.

The point of relative cohomology is to keep track of boundary integrals.
To characterize the non-trivial differential forms that generate boundary integrals produced by Stokes’ theorem when integrating over contours that have boundaries on the untwisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{B}_{t}
.
Since this is still an unfamiliar object for most of the physics community, we illustrate the formalism through an example instead of diving into mathematical definitions.
To this end, consider the two-site chain graph
G=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain.pdf}
:

\displaystyle S_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}

\displaystyle=X_{1}+Y_{12}

\displaystyle S_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}

\displaystyle=X_{2}+Y_{12}

\displaystyle S_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}

\displaystyle=X_{1}+X_{2}

(44)

Here,
\mathcal{B}
has three components:

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}
where, as usual,
\mathcal{B}_{t}=\mathsf{V}(B_{\tau})
and
B_{t}=\sum_{v\in V_{t}}x_{v}+S_{t}
.
This hyperplane arrangement is depicted figure
5
along with its cut spaces that have non-trivial cohomology.

\displaystyle M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/M12.pdf}

\displaystyle M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B12.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/M1_12.pdf}

\displaystyle M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2B12.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/M2_12.pdf}

\displaystyle M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B2.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/M1_2.pdf}

Figure 5:
The two-chain dual hyperplane arrangement:
\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{V}(x_{1}x_{2})}

with boundaries
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{B}}
(left).
The geometry of the cut spaces
M_{T}
(right).
Each of these geometries supports a 1-dimensional twisted cohomology; each has as single bounded chamber highlighted in light orange.
We always use the coboundary of the canonical forms associated to these bounded chambers as the basis for the boundary cohomology.

To illustrate the main features of the formalism, consider the dual integral

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\check{\varphi}\,,\qquad\check{\varphi}=\frac{\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\,,

(45)

where
\check{\gamma}
is a generic relative twisted contour that can have boundaries on
\mathcal{B}
(relative) and
\mathcal{T}
(twisted).
Note that while
\check{\varphi}
is a good dual form (regular on
\mathcal{B}
with poles on
T
), it is a total covariant derivative

\displaystyle\check{\varphi}=\frac{\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}=\check{\nabla}\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}-\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right)\,,

(46)

By Stokes theorem, the integral (
45
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\check{\nabla}\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}-\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right)=\sum_{t}\int_{\partial_{t}{\check{\gamma}}}\left.\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}-\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right|_{\mathcal{B}_{t}}\,,

(47)

where
\partial_{t}\check{\gamma}
computes the boundary of
\check{\gamma}
that lies in
\mathcal{B}_{t}
.
Eliminating
x_{1}
on the cut

\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}

and
x_{2}
on the

\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}

and
\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}

cuts yields

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\check{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial_{{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}{\check{\gamma}}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}})\;\Big(-\frac{\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\Big)+\int_{\partial_{{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}{\check{\gamma}}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}})\;\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right)

(48)

\displaystyle+\int_{\partial_{{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}{\check{\gamma}}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}})\;\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}-\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,(x_{1}+S_{3})}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=(-S_{1})^{-\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{-\alpha_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\check{\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}\,,

(49)

\displaystyle\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=(x_{1})^{-\alpha_{1}}(-S_{2})^{-\alpha_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\check{\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}\,,

\displaystyle\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=(x_{1})^{-\alpha_{1}}(-x_{1}-S_{3})^{-\alpha_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\check{\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}-\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,(x_{1}+S_{3})\,.

Next, note that these lower dimensional integrals on cuts can also be simplified

\displaystyle\text{on }\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}:

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=\frac{\check{\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}\left(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\text{on }\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}:

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=-\frac{\check{\omega}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}=\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}\left(-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right)\,,

(50)

\displaystyle\text{on }\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}:

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{1}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}{-}\alpha_{2}\;\mathrm{dlog}\,(x_{1}{+}S_{3})}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{=}\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+S_{3}}+\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}{-}\alpha_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2})}\right),

Substituting the above into (
48
), using Stokes theorem once again as well as
\partial_{t}\partial_{{t^{\prime}}}=-\partial_{{t^{\prime}}}\partial_{{t}}
, yields the fully reduced version of (
45
)

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\check{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\int_{\partial_{{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}\partial_{{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}{\check{\gamma}}}\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B2.pdf}}}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\int_{\partial_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}\partial_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}\check{\gamma}}\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B12.pdf}}}

(51)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\int_{\partial_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}\partial_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}\check{\gamma}}\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2B12.pdf}}}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\int_{\partial_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}{\check{\gamma}}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}})\;\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+S_{3}}

We have succeeded in simplifying the two dimensional integral (
45
) into three zero-dimensional integrals, and one one-dimensional integral.
The integrands are restricted to either the one-dimensional cut space
M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}
or the zero-dimensional cut spaces
M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B12.pdf}}
,
M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2B12.pdf}}
, and
M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B2.pdf}}
.
Moreover, on each cut, the integrands cannot be simplified further.
That is,
\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+S_{3}}
is a basis for
H^{1}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}};\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}\right)
and the constant function,
1
, is a basis for
H^{0}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B12.pdf}}\right)
,

H^{0}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2B12.pdf}}\right)
,
and

H^{0}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B12.pdf}}\right)
.

The discussion so far has relied on the interplay between forms and contours.
The relative twisted cohomology repackages everything so that we only need to talk only about differential forms on the cuts
M_{T}
and how the cuts/boundaries are coupled.
To accomplish this, we introduce the (sequential) coboundary symbol
\delta_{T}:=\delta_{t_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\delta_{t_{|T|}}
.
The coboundary, is defined so that

\displaystyle\int_{{\check{\gamma}}}\check{u}\;\delta_{T}(\check{\phi})=\int_{\partial_{t_{|T|}}\cdots\partial_{t_{1}}{\check{\gamma}}}(\check{u}|_{\mathcal{B}_{T}})\;\check{\phi}\,,

(52)

where
T=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{|T|})
.
For generic hyperplane arrangements, the coboundary is anti-symmetric in its indices
\delta_{tt^{\prime}}=-\delta_{t^{\prime}t}
.
This mirrors the anti-symmetry of the partial boundary operators
\partial_{t}\partial_{t^{\prime}}=-\partial_{t^{\prime}}\partial_{t}
.

Using this notation, (
51
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\check{\gamma}}\check{u}\;\check{\varphi}=\int_{{\check{\gamma}}}\check{u}\;\bigg[

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}(1)-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}(1)

(53)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}(1)+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}\left(\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+S_{3}}\right)\bigg]\,,

where

\displaystyle\left\{\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},}\left(\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+S_{3}}\right)\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}(1),\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}(1),\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}(1)\right\}\,,

(54)

is a basis of differential forms for the relative twisted cohomology
H^{2}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\nabla})
.
Note that
\delta_{t}
has form-degree one; all elements of (
54
) are to be thought of as relative twisted 2-forms.
By definition, the dual covariant derivative treats each
\delta_{t}
as a 1-form.
Moreover, whenever the derivative hits a coboundary symbol, it produces the corresponding boundary terms

\displaystyle\check{\nabla}\delta_{T}(\check{\phi})=(-1)^{|T|}\left[\delta_{T}(\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{T}}\check{\phi})+\sum_{t\notin T}\delta_{T\cup t}(\check{\phi}|_{\mathcal{B}_{T\cup t}})\right]\,.

(55)

Also, the absence of a coboundary symbol is equivalent to
\delta_{\emptyset}
.
In this way, boundary terms are still generated when acting with the covariant derivative on forms without any
\delta_{\bullet}
’s.

We can now declare that
\check{\varphi}
is cohomologous to the combination of forms in (
51
).
Explicitly,

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}-\check{\nabla}\text{IBP}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}(1)+\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}(1)

(56)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}(1)+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}\left(\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{1}+S_{3}}\right)\,,

where

\displaystyle\text{IBP}{=}\frac{\alpha_{1}\,\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{1}{-}\alpha_{2}\,\mathrm{dlog}\,x_{2}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{-}\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}(1){+}\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}(1){-}\frac{\alpha_{2}{-}\alpha_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2})}\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}(1).

(57)

A general feature of the relative twisted cohomology is that it has a decomposition into the direct sum of the twisted cohomologies on each cut (for generic arrangements)

\displaystyle H^{|V_{G}|}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\nabla}):=\bigoplus_{T\text{ (cuts)}}\delta_{T}\circ H^{|V_{G}|-|T|}(M_{T};\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{T}})\,.

(58)

That is, relative twisted
|V_{G}|
-forms are vectors whose components (labeled by the
\delta_{\bullet}
) are twisted
(|V_{G}|-|T|)
-forms on
M_{T}
.
Like the FRW-cohomology, there is a middle dimensional theorem and only
H^{|V_{G}|}\neq 0
.
For the two-site chain, this decomposition is

\displaystyle H^{2}(M,\mathcal{B};\check{\nabla})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}\circ H^{1}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}};\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf}}}\right)\oplus\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}\circ H^{0}(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B2.pdf}})

(59)

\displaystyle\oplus\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}\circ H^{0}(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2B12.pdf}})\oplus\delta_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}\circ H^{0}(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1B12.pdf}})\,,

where

H^{2}(M;\check{\nabla})=H^{1}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}};\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}}\right)=H^{1}\left(M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}};\check{\nabla}|_{\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}}\right)=0

since there are no bounded chambers on
M
,

M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B1.pdf}}
,
and
M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/2chain_B2.pdf}}
.
Moreover, each
M_{T}
in (
58
) is a (twisted) hyperplane and therefore a positive geometry.
To find a basis for the relative twisted cohomology, we use the coboundary of the canonical forms on each
M_{T}
.

Sum of
positive geometries!

Equation (
58
) says that the dual cohomology is built up from the cohomology of the cuts
M_{T}
.
On each cut there is a (twisted) hyperplane arrangement which is a positive geometry.
Hence, the dual cohomology is simply the direct sum of the cohomology associated to these positive geometries!

We will build all FRW- and dual-forms from the positive geometry of the cuts (
94
) and show that this is equivalent to a simple combinatorial formula (
88
).

Via the intersection duality, the FRW-cohomology can be organized by cuts as schematically shown in (
41
).

Cuts from the coboundary and the intersection number.

The last but most important part property of the coboundary is that it induces a (generalized unitarity) cut in the intersection number

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{T}(\check{\phi})|\phi\rangle=\langle\check{\phi}|\text{Res}_{T}[\phi]\rangle\,,

(60)

where
\text{Res}_{T}:=\text{Res}_{B_{t_{|T|}}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{t_{1}}}
.
Note that when
\phi
has higher order poles, the above formula must be modified slightly
Caron-Huot:2021xqj
;
Caron-Huot:2021iev
.
13
13

13

When there are higher order poles,
\text{Res}_{T}[\bullet]\to\text{Res}_{T}[\frac{u|_{\mathcal{B}_{T}}}{u}\bullet]
.

The intersection number also turns the remaining part of the dual form
\check{\phi}
in (
60
) into another residue operator acting on
\text{Res}_{T}[\phi]

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{T}(\check{\phi})|\phi\rangle=\langle\check{\phi}|\text{Res}_{T}[\phi]\rangle=\text{Res}_{\check{\phi}}\circ\text{Res}_{T}[\phi].

(61)

Here,
\text{Res}_{\check{\phi}}
localizes to where
\check{\phi}
is maximally singular.
We avoid the details to construct
\text{Res}_{\check{\phi}}
(the interested reader is encouraged to look at
Caron-Huot:2021iev
;
Caron-Huot:2021xqj
and references therein).
Instead, we provide an explicit formula for
\text{Res}_{\check{\phi}}
in the context of our basis forms in section
5
.

In this paper, one should think of dual forms simply as an an instruction for how to construct residue operators that project FRW-integrals onto a basis.
Given a basis
\{\phi_{a}\}
for the FRW-cohomology and a basis
\{\check{\phi}_{a}\}
for the dual cohomology, the matrix elements of the differential equation are

\displaystyle A_{ab}=C_{bc}^{-1}\langle\check{\phi}_{c}|\text{d}_{\text{kin}}\phi_{a}\rangle

(62)

where
C_{ab}=\langle\check{\phi}_{a}|\phi_{b}\rangle
is the intersection matrix.
Closed formula for both the intersection matrix
\underline{\bm{C}}
and the connection
\underline{\bm{A}}
are located in section
5
.

3
Constructing the cut basis

In section
3.1
, we identify the degeneracy in the cosmological hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{B}
and deduce its implications on sequential residues and logarithmic forms.
Then, we introduce graphical/combinatorial gadgets (acyclic minors) to help organize the structure of the basis and resulting differential equations in section
3.2
.
Next, the cut basis is constructed directly from the acyclic minors (section
3.3
) and related to the canonical form of the positive geometry on each physical cut (
3.4
).
Lastly, in section
3.5
, we discuss how the cut basis is related to the time integral basis of
Baumann:2025qjx
;
He:2024olr
.

3.1
Independent cuts of the physical FRW-form

It is well-known that the kinematic derivative of a Feynman integral can be written as a linear combination of itself and other Feynman integrals where one propagator (pole in the integrand) is removed.
Usually, a family of Feynman integrals is classified according to sectors that are defined by which propagators (poles) are present in the integrand. A similar approach carries over to the FRW-integrals we consider here. The kinematic differential of an FRW-integral cannot couple to another FRW integral unless the set of propagators of the second is a subset of the first.
This is a direct consequence of equations (
60
), (
61
) and (
62
), and the fact that a kinematic derivative cannot introduce new poles that were not already present.
Therefore, the structure of the differential equation is controlled by the independent cuts/residues of physical FRW-form.

Recall from (
29
) and (
35
) that the physical FRW-form is given simply by

\displaystyle\Psi_{G}:=\hat{\Omega}_{G}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},{\bf Y})\;\text{d}^{|V_{G}|}\mathbf{x}=\prod_{e\in E_{G}}2Y_{e}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{G}^{\text{max}}}\frac{1}{B_{\tau}}\text{d}^{|V_{G}|}\mathbf{x}\,.

(63)

It is important to emphasize that
|\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{max}}_{G}|=|V_{G}|+|E_{G}|
, which is larger than
|V_{G}|
, the number of integration variables
x_{i}
.
Therefore, it is possible to use partial fractions such that each term in
\Psi_{G}
has at most
|V_{G}|
factors in the denominator.
Graphical/combinatorial algorithms for such a decomposition are discussed in
Fevola:2024nzj
;
Glew:2025arc
;
Glew:2025ugf
.
For example, performing partial fractions on the shifted version of the flat-space wavefunction coefficient associated to the 3-site chain, (
31
), yields

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hat{\Omega}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3}}({\bf x}+{\bf X},{\bf Y})&=\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t1_b}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t2}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t3}}}}+\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t4_b}}}+\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t5_b}}}\\
&-\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line_t1_b}}}-\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line_t1_b}}}-\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line_t1_b}}}-\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line_t1_b}}}+\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_point_1.pdf}}}\,.\end{aligned}

(64)

Naively, one expects that after repeated use of partial fractions, so that each term in (
63
) has
|V_{G}|
factors in the denominator, the resulting terms would expose all possible maximal cuts/residues of
\Psi_{G}
.
Continuing the example (
64
), we expect to have
10
distinct maximal cuts.
However, this misses the fact that not all terms are linearly independent!
Indeed, the
blue
terms in (
64
) satisfy the linear relation

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t2}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t3}}}}=\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_o1}}}+\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_o2}}}\,,

(65)

since

\displaystyle B_{\begin{gathered}\hbox to20.93pt{\vbox to7.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.1044pt\lower-3.81435pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{{}}

The origin of this linear dependence is geometric.
The untwisted hyperplane arrangement
\mathcal{B}
is degenerate for
|V_{G}|>2
: there are codimension-
k
surfaces in
\mathcal{B}
where more than
k
hyperplanes intersect.
This is due to the hyperplane polynomials
B_{\tau}
satisfying
(n\geq 4)
-term identities
14
14

14

Note that
\tau_{1}\cap\tau_{2}
may correspond to more than one tube; in such cases,
B_{\tau_{1}\cap\tau_{2}}:=\sum_{\tau\in\tau_{1}\cap\tau_{2}}B_{\tau}
.

\displaystyle B_{\tau_{1}}+B_{\tau_{2}}=B_{\tau_{1}\cap\tau_{2}}+B_{\tau_{1}\cup\tau_{2}}\,.

(70)

Consequently, there are relations between sequential residue operators since setting any three

) to zero automatically sets the fourth to zero.

To derive relations between residue operators, one needs to fix an ordering for the degenerate tubes in (
70
)
dupont2015orliksolomonmodelhypersurfacearrangements
;
orlik1980combinatorics
.
If
\{b_{k}\}_{k=1}^{m}
is the set of tubes that appear in (
70
) and they are ordered
(b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
, we have

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{b_{m-1}=0}\circ\text{Res}_{b_{m-2}=0}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{b_{1}=0}=\text{Res}_{b_{m}=0}\circ\text{Res}_{b_{m-2}=0}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{b_{1}=0}\,.

(75)

We also have the following relation among logarithmic forms

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{m-i}\bigwedge_{j\neq i}\mathrm{dlog}b_{j}=0\,.

(76)

Importantly, the coboundary symbols
\delta_{\bullet}
in the dual-forms also satisfy the same relations as the corresponding residues operators.

Returning to the 3-site chain example, and ordering the tubes via

\displaystyle\left(\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf},\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}\right)

(77)

reveals that there are only three linearly independent sequential residues that respect this tube ordering

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \{\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}&=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}\,,\\
\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}&\,,\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}}\circ\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}}\}\,.\end{aligned}

(78)

Moreover, equation (
65
) follows form (
76
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}-\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}\\
&\quad+\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}-\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\,\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}=0\,,\end{aligned}

(79)

after factoring out the volume form
\text{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}
.

Cut ordering

The cut multi-index
J
in
\delta_{J}
,
\text{Res}_{J}
and
M_{J}
is
always
ordered such that tubes with the most vertices comes first.
For tubes of the same size, we proceed in lexicographical order.

This choice is natural in the sense that sequential residues with crossed tubes vanish respecting the compatibility of tubes established earlier.
In particular, all residues with crossed tubes annihilate the physical FRW-form when ordered this way.

An algorithm for enumerating all independent cuts of the physical FRW-form
\Psi_{G}
using
cut tubings
was provided in
De:2024zic
. However, this approach requires careful bookkeeping of degeneracies between cuts arising from linear relations such as (
70
). In this work, we improve the algorithm by demonstrating how the
acyclic minors
introduced in
Glew:2025ugf
;
He:2024olr
capture the set of independent cuts of
\Psi_{G}
, without encountering the issue of degeneracy.

3.2
Acyclic minors

To label the set of independent cuts of the physical FRW-form we introduce the notion of an
acyclic minor
. An acyclic minor of a graph
G
is a decorated graph
\mathfrak{g}
, obtained by assigning to each edge
e\in E_{G}
one of three decorations:
(solid),
(broken) or

(oriented), such that the oriented graph resulting from contracting all solid edges and deleting all broken edges is acyclic in the usual sense. For example, the complete set of acyclic minors for the three-site chain and two-cycle are illustrated in figure
2
and figure
6
.
We use
E_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\hbox to10.17pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.1pt\lower-1.1pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.02 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.02 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.45 0 C 1.45 0.8 0.8 1.45 0 1.45 C -0.8 1.45 -1.45 0.8 -1.45 0 C -1.45 -0.8 -0.8 -1.45 0 -1.45 C 0.8 -1.45 1.45 -0.8 1.45 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.02 0 M 12.48 0 C 12.48 0.8 11.83 1.45 11.02 1.45 C 10.22 1.45 9.57 0.8 9.57 0 C 9.57 -0.8 10.22 -1.45 11.02 -1.45 C 11.83 -1.45 12.48 -0.8 12.48 0 Z M 11.02 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
to denote the set of solid edges of
\mathfrak{g}
, and similarly

(80)

In this context,
\mathcal{A}_{E}(G)
denotes the set of acyclic minors with fixed broken edge set
E
and
\mathcal{A}_{E}^{\emptyset}(G)
is the subset with fixed broken edge set
E
and no solid edges.

Unlike the cut tubings of
De:2024zic
, the set of acyclic minors is in one-to-one correspondence with the independent cuts of the physical FRW-form. Each acyclic minor
\mathfrak{g}
determines a set
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
of cut tubings.
If the cut is degenerate, then
|\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}|>1
. Otherwise, the acyclic minor
\mathfrak{g}
corresponds to a unique cut tubing.

Given an acyclic minor
\mathfrak{g}
, the set of associated cut tubings
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is obtained by the following simple recipe:

1.

Contract all solid edges and delete all broken edges of the decorated graph
\mathfrak{g}
.

2.

On the resulting oriented graph, identify all vertex-induced
15
15

15

A vertex-induced tube of a graph
G
is a connected subgraph formed by a subset of vertices
V\subset V_{G}
, along with all edges of
G
whose endpoints are both contained in
V
.
tubes that have no incoming oriented edges from vertices outside the tube.

3.

Uplift each tube back to the original decorated graph by reversing the contractions and reinstating the deleted edges.

4.

Form all maximal sets of compatible tubes from this collection to obtain the set of cut tubings
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(81)

The resulting set of tubes contain no broken edges, do not cross any solid edges and have all oriented edges ‘flowing outwards’ from the tubes.

Figure 6:
The
10
acyclic minors of the two-cycle.

Letters.

A letter of
\mathfrak{g}
is defined to be any connected subgraph consisting of only solid edges, together with all incident incoming (half) oriented edges. We denote by
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}
the set of letters associated to
\mathfrak{g}
, and represent each letter by a coloring on the graph, as illustrated in the following examples

(82)

For each letter
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}
we denote the associated vertex set by
V_{\mathfrak{l}}
, which is assumed to be in ascending order.

Additionally, the set of letters
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is ordered such that
\mathfrak{l}_{i}<\mathfrak{l}_{j}
whenever
\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}<\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{j}}
where
\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}:=\min V_{\mathfrak{l}}
.
This ordering is introduced here for future convenience where it is used to keep track of signs generated by the wedge product.

Crucially, each letter defines a function of the graph variables and we will use the terminology letters interchangeably for both the colored subgraph and their associated functions. To read off the letter associated to a colored subgraph we simply: sum over
X_{v}
for all colored vertices, subtract
Y_{e}
for each colored half edge, and sum over
Y_{e}
for each uncolored edge incident to a colored vertex, schematically that is
16
16

16

Here we emphasize the second sum is over only those uncolored edges incident to a colored vertex.

\displaystyle f_{\mathfrak{l}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}X_{v}+\sum_{e:\text{uncolored}}Y_{e}-\sum_{e:\text{colored}}Y_{e}\,.

(83)

82

(84)

These letters serve two important purposes: they appear as arguments of the kinematic
\mathrm{dlog}
’s in the differential equations, and, their shifted versions control the geometry of the physical cuts, as detailed in section
3.4
.

Mergers.

We say a pair of acyclic minors
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
are related by a
merger
if
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\mathfrak{g}
by replacing all oriented edges between two letters of
\mathfrak{g}
with solid edges, this follows the terminology introduced in
Arkani-Hamed:2023bsv
;
Baumann:2025qjx
. Moreover, for any pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
related by a merger, their set of letters can be organized as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}\cup\{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}\}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\uparrow},

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}=\{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}:\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}<\overline{V}_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}\cup\{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\downarrow},\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\uparrow}\}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}=\{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}:\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}>\overline{V}_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}\}\,.

(85)

Whenever this is the case, it follows that for each
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, there exists a unique
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
\tau^{\prime}\subset\tau
.

In deriving the off-diagonal components of the differential equation (section (
5.1
) and appendix
B.3
), it will be useful to define the set of such pairs of cut tubings by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}=\{(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}:\tau^{\prime}\subset\tau\}.

(86)

For example, the acyclic minors appearing in (
3.2
) are related by a merger, and we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\raisebox{-2.56064pt}{\hbox to25.96pt{\vbox to7.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.6pt\lower-3.95583pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

(87)

In this case, the letters are organized as:
\mathbb{L}^{\downarrow}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\emptyset
,
\underline{\mathfrak{l}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}X_{1}+X_{2}+Y_{23}}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\downarrow}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}X_{1}-Y_{12}}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\uparrow}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}X_{2}+Y_{12}+Y_{23}}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\uparrow}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}X_{3}-Y_{23}}\}
.

3.3
Cut basis

In this section we explain how to associate an FRW-form to each acyclic minor, then in section
3.4
, we explain the positive geometric origin of this basis.

Given an acyclic minor
\mathfrak{g}
, we define the form

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau},

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{dlog}_{\tau}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\bigwedge_{v\in V_{G}}\mathrm{dlog}\begin{cases}B_{\langle v\rangle_{\tau}}\text{ if }v\in r,\\
x_{v}\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

(88)

where the sum appearing on the right is over the cartesian product of the vertex sets of letters

\mathcal{R}_{\mathfrak{g}}=V_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\times\ldots\times V_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}.

In addition, given a vertex
v\in V_{G}
and tube
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we define
\langle v\rangle_{\tau}
to be the smallest tube
t\in\tau
such that
v\in V_{t}
. We refer to the set of forms in (
88
) as the cut basis, which is closely related to the cut basis introduced in
De:2024zic
, differing only by overall signs.
Continuing our running examples for the three-site chain we find the following elements of the cut basis

\displaystyle\phi_{\raisebox{-2.27626pt}{\hbox to25.96pt{\vbox to7.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.6pt\lower-3.95583pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.6397pt}{-1.62271pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
