(90)

Following the results of
Glew:2025ugf
;
Glew:2025arc
it is clear that the physical FRW-form has a simple expansion in terms of the cut basis

\displaystyle\Psi_{G}=\sum_{E\subset E_{G}}(-1)^{|E|}\sum_{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{A}^{\emptyset}_{E}(G)}\ \phi_{\mathfrak{g}},

(91)

As a simple example, the wavefunction for the path graph on three vertices is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Psi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t1}}=&\left(\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t2}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t3}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t4}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t5}}\right)\\
-&\left(\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t8}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t9}}\right)\\
-&\left(\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t6}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t7}}\right)\\
+&\left(\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t10}}\right)\,.\end{aligned}

(92)

An alternative set of forms
\{\tilde{\phi}_{G}\}
, sharing similar properties with the cut basis, was recently proposed in
Capuano:2025ehm
. However, the degeneracy of the hyperplane arrangement was not taken into account, so that in general the cardinality
|\{\tilde{\phi}_{G}\}|
is greater than that of the cut basis.

Organizing the wedge product.

When deriving the differential equations it will prove useful to rewrite (
88
) using the notation of
De:2024zic
.
To do this, note that
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
can be re-written as

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}\left(\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\bigwedge_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}B_{\langle\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}\rangle_{\tau}}\right)\wedge\overset{\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}}{\overbrace{\left(\bigwedge_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}\right)}}\,,\qquad\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}=\bigwedge_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}\setminus\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{x_{v}}{x_{\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}}}\,,

(93)

where
\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\mathrm{sig}(\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}},\dots,\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}},V_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\setminus\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}},\dots,V_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\setminus\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}})

and the two wedge products are ordered such that
\min V_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}<\min V_{\mathfrak{l}_{i+1}}
for all
\mathfrak{l}_{i}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Note, when
|V_{\mathfrak{l}}|=1
we have

\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}=1
.
Moreover, as discussed in the next section,
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}
are closely related the canonical forms of positive geometries.

Already in (
93
), the cut structure is
made manifest by the two terms in brackets; the first has the potential to be cut while the second becomes the canonical form on the maximal cut.
However, to isolate the physical cuts, we had to choose a global ordering for the
B_{\bullet}
.
Implementing this ordering on the
B_{\bullet}
introduces an additional compensating sign

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\text{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C_{\mathfrak{g}}}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\text{sgn}_{\tau}\bigwedge_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mathrm{dlog}B_{\sigma(\langle\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}\rangle_{\tau})},

(94)

where
\sigma
is the permutation that implements the global ordering and

\mathrm{sgn}_{\tau}:=\mathrm{sig}(\langle\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\rangle_{\tau},\dots,\allowbreak\langle\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\rangle_{\tau})/\mathrm{sig}(\sigma(\langle\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\rangle_{\tau}),\dots,\sigma(\langle\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\rangle_{\tau}))
.
Note that
\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\mathrm{sgn}_{\tau}
are there to undo signs generated by reordering the wedge product in (
88
) and will drop out of the final expression for the differential equations.

3.4
Positive geometry of
physical
cuts

We now seek to motivate the definition of the cut basis in (
88
) by examining the positive geometry associated to physical cuts. Since each acyclic minor corresponds uniquely to a physical cut, it is convenient to introduce the notation
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathcal{B}_{\tau}
for
\tau\in\mathcal{C_{\mathfrak{g}}}
, where, by construction, we have
\mathcal{B}_{\tau}=\mathcal{B_{\tau^{\prime}}}
for all
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Each cut space,

M_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}=(\mathbb{CP}^{|V_{G}|}\setminus\mathcal{T})\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}},

is the complement of a hyperplane arrangement with a
single bounded chamber
carved out by the restriction of the twisted divisors to
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. This bounded chamber
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
is an example of a positive geometry which turns out to have a product structure given by
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}=\bigtimes_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\Gamma_{\mathfrak{l}}
where each component is a simplex:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{l}}

\displaystyle:=\{(x_{v_{1}},\cdots,x_{v_{|V_{\mathfrak{l}}|}})\in\mathbb{R}^{|V_{\mathfrak{l}}|}:x_{v}\leq 0\;\text{for all }v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}\text{ and }f_{\mathfrak{l}}({\bf x}+{\bf X},{\bf Y})=0\}\,.

(95)

Moreover, the maximal cut of
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
is exactly the canonical form
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
of the bounded chamber
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
, that is

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}=\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}\,,

(96)

where

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\text{sgn}_{\tau}\,\text{Res}_{\tau}\,,

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\tau}

\displaystyle:=\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{t_{|\tau|}}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{B}_{t_{1}}}\,,

(97)

is the residue operator for both non-degenerate and degenerate cuts.

To see why each cut has a geometry described by (
95
), consider the following acyclic minor of the double box graph together with its cut conditions

\mathfrak{g}=\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt_tube.pdf}

\displaystyle-(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}{\displaystyle X_{1}+X_{2}+X_{3}+Y_{14}+Y_{25}+Y_{36}}\,,

(98)

\displaystyle-(x_{4}+x_{5}+x_{6})=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\displaystyle X_{4}+X_{5}+X_{6}+Y_{14}+Y_{25}+Y_{36}}\,,

\displaystyle-x_{4}=

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,1,0}{\displaystyle X_{4}+Y_{14}+Y_{45}}\,.

The bounded chamber
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
, is

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{6}:x_{i}\leq 0\text{ and }\eqref{eq:double_examp1}\text{ is satisfied}\}\,.

(99)

To see the Minkowski decomposition of this region, combine the
green
cut condition with the
red
cut condition.
This yields a new but equivalent linear system in terms of letters

\displaystyle f_{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt.pdf}
}}}}}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+X_{1}+X_{2}+X_{3}+Y_{14}+Y_{25}+Y_{36}}=0\,,

(100)

\displaystyle f_{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt_1.pdf}
}}}}}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x_{5}+x_{6}+X_{5}+X_{6}+Y_{25}+Y_{36}-Y_{45}}=0\,,

\displaystyle f_{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt_copy_2.pdf}
}}}}}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,1,0}x_{4}+X_{4}+Y_{14}+Y_{45}}=0\,.

Each equation above places a constraint on a subset of the integration variables, which together partition
(x_{1},\dots,x_{6})
.
Therefore, we can interpret each line of equation (
100
) as a 2-, 1- and 0-simplex, respectively

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt.pdf}
}}}}}\times\Gamma_{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt_1.pdf}
}}}}}\times\Gamma_{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/double_box_exam_alt_copy_2.pdf}
}}}}}\,.

(101)

Moreover, since the left-hand-sides of (
100
) partition the integration variables, each simplex is orthogonal to the others.
Thus, the Minkowski sum of these simplices is equivalent to the Cartesian product.
In the context of the above example, the Cartesian product makes a triangular prism:

\displaystyle\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/toblerone.pdf}\ .

(102)

3.5
Relation to the time integral basis

It should be noted that the basis of forms presented here is closely related to the
time integral basis
recently proposed in
Baumann:2025qjx
. As an example, for the path graph on two vertices, the cut basis is given by

\displaystyle\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t2}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}}{(X_{1}+x_{1}+Y_{12})(X_{1}+X_{2}+x_{1}+x_{2})},

\displaystyle\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t3}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}}{(X_{1}+X_{2}+x_{1}+x_{2})(X_{2}+x_{2}+Y_{12})},

\displaystyle\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t4}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}}{(X_{1}+x_{1}+Y_{12})(X_{2}+x_{2}+Y_{12})},

\displaystyle\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t1}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}}\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}}{(X_{1}+X_{2}+x_{1}+x_{2})}.

(103)

The time integral basis on the other hand includes the first three forms above together with

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t1}}

\displaystyle=\int\text{d}x_{1}\wedge\text{d}x_{2}\frac{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}}{(X_{1}+X_{2}+x_{1}+x_{2})^{2}}.

(104)

However, it is easy to show that these two forms differ by a total derivative given explicitly by

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\phi^{\prime}_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t1}}-\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_2_t1}}=\nabla\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}}\text{d}x_{1}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{1}+x_{2}+X_{1}+X_{2}}\right).

(105)

As demonstrated by the above example, when the acyclic minor contains no solid edges, the cut basis and time integral basis forms coincide.
More generally, by comparing the structure of our differential equations (c.f., (
128
) and (
132
), or (
138
)) to those of
Baumann:2025qjx
, suggests that the cut basis and time integral basis are equivalent up to total derivatives and a rescaling.

4
Zonotopes and the flow of cuts

It has recently been observed that
graphical zonotopes
play an important role in characterising the structure of differential equations for the wavefunction
Baumann:2025qjx
. These zonotopes (defined as Minkowski sums of line segments) naturally arise by considering the representation of the (flat-space) wavefunction in terms of cut tubings. To illustrate this, consider the following subset of terms in the wavefunction for the three-site chain

\displaystyle\hat{\Omega}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3.pdf}}\supset{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t1.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t2.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t3.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t4.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_t5.pdf}}}}.

(106)

The acyclic minors

\mathfrak{g}\in\{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_X_g.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_X_b.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_X_r.pdf},\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/pt3_X_o.pdf}\}
which label each set of (degenerate) cut tubings appearing in (
106
) have the property
|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|=|V_{G}|
. As a result, solving for
{\bf X}
in the equations
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}=0
yields a unique point
{\bf X}^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|V_{G}|}
. In this example, by solving
\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}=0
for each acyclic minor, we produce the four points

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_g.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_g_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_g_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_g_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3},

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_b.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_b_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_b_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_b_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3},

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_r.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_r_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_r_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_r_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3},

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_o.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_o_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_o_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_o_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3}.

(107)

The unit vector
\hat{e}_{i}
, in the
i
-th vertex direction, appears above multiplied by the unique letter whose vertex set contains
v_{i}
. By taking the convex hull of these four points we generate a zonotope (parallelogram) which resides on the total energy facet within the cosmological hyperplane arrangement (see figure
8
).

Figure 7:
The four zonotopes for the three-site chain.
These are certain projections of the positive region of the hyperplane arrangement. Each line/point represents the intersection of the hyperplanes labeled by the adjacent tubes.
The right most zonotope resides on the total energy facet
X_{1}+X_{2}+X_{3}=0
and is bounded by five planes. Due to degeneracy, the five planes intersect the total energy facet in a square rather than a pentagon. The two middle zonotopes live on the cut surfaces
X_{1}+X_{2}+Y_{23}=X_{3}+Y_{23}=0
and
X_{1}+Y_{12}=X_{2}+X_{3}+Y_{12}=0
respectively, both producing line segments. The final zonotope is a point at
X_{1}+Y_{12}=X_{2}+Y_{12}+Y_{23}=X_{3}+Y_{23}=0
.

This procedure can be performed for each subset of terms in the partial fraction expression for the wavefunction that contain the same set of maximal (by inclusion) tubes. The four zonotopes generated in this way for the three-site chain are illustrated in figure
7
. For example, taking the points generated by either of the subsets

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hat{\Omega}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3.pdf}}\supset&-\left({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line2_t1.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line2_t2.pdf}}}}\right)\quad\text{ or }\quad\hat{\Omega}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3.pdf}}\supset&-\left({\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line_t1.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.47,0.62}\frac{1}{S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_line_t2.pdf}}}}\right)\,,\end{aligned}

(108)

we obtain two line segments defined as the convex hull of the pairs of points

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_M.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_M_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_M_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_M_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3},

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Gr.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Gr_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Gr_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Gr_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3},

or

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Cy.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Cy_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Cy_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Cy_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3},

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Sa.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Sa_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Sa_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Sa_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3}.

(109)

Finally, for the remaining term

1/S_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_point_1.pdf}}
, we generate a single point

\displaystyle-{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Y.pdf}}=f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Y_l1.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{1}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Y_l2.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{2}+f_{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3_X_Y_l3.pdf}}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\ \hat{e}_{3}.

(110)

The above discussion can be generalized to arbitrary graphs. For each acyclic minor labeling a set of terms appearing in (
91
) we define the point

\displaystyle{\bf X}^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\sum_{v\in V_{G}}-f_{\mathfrak{l}_{v}}({\bf 0},{\bf Y})\hat{e}_{v},

(111)

where
\mathfrak{l}_{v}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is the unique letter containing
v
in its vertex set. This allows us to define a family of zonotopes for each graph
G
: one for each subset of edges
E\subset E_{G}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}=\text{conv}\left\{{\bf X}^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{A}_{E}^{\emptyset}(G)\right\},

(112)

where
\mathfrak{g}(E)
is the acyclic minor associated to the bulk of the polytope—the acyclic minor with broken edges
E
and all remaining edges solid. For instance, the four zonotopes depicted in figure
7
for the three-site chain are denoted by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cc.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t2}},{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t3}},{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t4}},{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t5}}\},

\displaystyle\text{{(square in figure \ref{fig:zonoExample}),}}

(113)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cb.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t8}},{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t9}}\},

\displaystyle\text{{(line in figure \ref{fig:zonoExample}),}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bc.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t6}},{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t7}}\},

\displaystyle\text{{(line in figure \ref{fig:zonoExample}),}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bb.pdf}}

\displaystyle=\text{conv}\{{\bf X}^{*}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/amp_t10}}\},

\displaystyle\text{{(point in figure \ref{fig:zonoExample})}}.

Alternatively, given an edge
e\in E_{G}
with end points
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
, we define the
point

\Delta_{ij}^{+}
and
line segment

\Delta_{ij}^{-}
as

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}_{e}=-Y_{e}(\hat{e}_{i}+\hat{e}_{j}),\quad\quad\Delta^{-}_{e}=\text{conv}\left\{Y_{e}(\hat{e}_{i}-\hat{e}_{j}),Y_{e}(\hat{e}_{j}-\hat{e}_{i})\right\}.

(114)

With this notation the zonotope (
112
) can be written as the following (translated) Minkowski sum of line segments

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}=\bigoplus_{e\in E_{G}}\begin{cases}\Delta^{+}_{e}&\text{if }e\in E,\\
\Delta^{-}_{e}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(115)

Remarkably, the set of faces associated to this family of zonotopes are in one-to-one correspondence with the independent cuts of the physical FRW-form and (by construction) with the elements of the cut basis used to formulate our differential equations. This provides a geometric origin for the appearance of zonotopes in the differential equations of
Baumann:2025qjx
.

Flow of cuts/sequential residues.

The zonotope structure observed for the flat-space wavefunction
\hat{\Omega}_{G}
straightforwardly carries over to the physical FRW-form
\Psi_{G}:=\hat{\Omega}_{G}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\text{d}^{|V_{G}|}\mathbf{x}
and the arrangement of untwisted hyperplanes
\mathcal{B}
.
With the coordinates that specify the vertices of the zonotopes now given by

\displaystyle{\bf x}^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}=\sum_{v\in V_{G}}-f_{\mathfrak{l}_{v}}({\bf X},{\bf Y})\hat{e}_{v}.

(116)

Moreover, the residues of the physical FRW-form
\Psi_{G}
are the canonical form of the appropriate zonotope (or its boundaries).
To see this, we recall equation (
91
) here for convenience

\displaystyle\Psi_{G}=\sum_{E\subset E_{G}}(-1)^{|E|}\sum_{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{A}^{\emptyset}_{E}(G)}\ \phi_{\mathfrak{g}}.

(117)

Each set of acyclic minors
\mathcal{A}_{E}^{\emptyset}(G)
with fixed broken edges and no solid edges is compatible with the cut
\mathcal{C}_{g(E)}
. Therefore, taking the sequential residue
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}}
of the physical FRW-form annihilates all terms that do not have the same set of broken edges.
The resulting sum is the canonical form of the zonotope
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}
:
17
17

17

We note that a similar observation was made by Harry Goodhew during the online Universe+ seminar
U+HarryTalk
.

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}}[\Psi_{G}]=\sum_{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{A}^{\emptyset}_{E}(G)}\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}}[\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}]=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\mathfrak{g}(E)}]\,.

(118)

If the hyperplane arrangement of the untwisted diviors
\mathcal{B}
were generic, this statement would be obvious since each
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{A}^{\emptyset}_{E}(G)
corresponds to a vertex of
\mathcal{Z}_{g(E)}
.
However, since
\mathcal{B}
is degenerate, we must check that the cut basis correctly assigns
\mathrm{dlog}
-forms to the degenerate vertices of the zonotopes. Choosing the correct prescription for the
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
where
\mathfrak{g}
corresponds to a set of degenerate tubings was already solved in section
3
and
De:2024zic
with the prescription for
\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
in (
94
) (we will revisit this below in the context of three-site chain).

Flow of cuts

From (
118
) it is clear that the combinatorics of further sequential residues of
\Psi_{G}
is determined by the geometry of the zonotopes through the canonical form map.
The sequential residue
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}
can flow to
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{g}
labels a boundary of the facet labeled by
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
; equivalently when
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
are related by a merger (c.f., section
3.2
).
We call this combinatorics the
flow of cuts
.

Figure 8:
The arrangement of untwisted divisors on the total energy plane of the 3-site chain graph
(not the cut space
M_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cc.pdf}}
).
The zonotope is the
red
shaded region, the
cyan
lines are the intersection of the total energy plane and the remaining untwisted planes.
The dots in are the intersection of the total energy plane with at least two other untwisted planes.
The
magenta
dots are vertices of the zonotope where the physical form has non-trivial triple residues while at the
gray
dots, the physical form has trivial residue.
On the total energy cut, the physical form
\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3.pdf}}
is proportional to the canonical form of the zonotope.

To make the above discussion concrete, consider the three-site chain as an example. The arrangement of the untwisted divisors on the total energy plane is depicted in figure
8
(left).
The
red
shaded region is identified with a zonotope whose top-dimensional facet is labeled by the acyclic minor with only solid edges.
The codimension-one boundaries (
cyan
) are also labeled by acyclic minors with one solid edge; they correspond to the intersection of the planes associated to tubes attached to the acyclic minor.
Lastly, the vertices (
magenta
) of the zonotope are labeled by acyclic minors with only directed edges; again, these correspond to the intersection of the planes associated to tubes attached to the acyclic minor.

To compute the canonical form of a region with degenerate facets, the arrangement must be perturbed in order to become generic. This is illustrated in figure
8
(right) where we shift the line

\mathsf{V}(B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}})\cap\mathsf{V}(B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}})
. The canonical form of the degenerate arrangement is then obtained as the limit of the canonical form of the generic arrangement.
Note that in the generic arrangement, there are two vertices labeled by the cut tubings associated to the acyclic minor
. The contribution of these two vertices to the canonical form is given by

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}[\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t3}}]=\left.\left[{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}}B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\frac{1}{B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}}B_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}}}}\right]\right|_{B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}=0}\text{d}^{2}\mathbf{x}_{\parallel}\,,

(119)

where

\mathbf{x}_{\parallel}
are coordinates on the total energy plane

\mathcal{B}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}
and we recall that

\displaystyle\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t3}}=\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t3}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\frac{B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B23.pdf}}}{B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B12.pdf}}}\wedge\mathrm{dlog}B_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B2.pdf}}\,.

(120)

Therefore,

\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/delta_cc.pdf}}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3.pdf}}]=\text{Res}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/3chain_B123.pdf}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/pt3.pdf}}]
, is the canonical form of the zonotope

\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cc.pdf}}

even though the point
{\bf x}^{*}_{\includegraphics[align,scale]{figures/amp_t3}}
is degenerate. We conclude that the definition of
\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
in (
94
) is compatible with the interpretation of the canonical form for zonotopes with degenerate facets.

The remaining zonotopes are generic and it is easily verified that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cb.pdf}}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cb.pdf}}}[\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t8}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t9}}]=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_cb.pdf}}]\,,\\
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bc.pdf}}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bc.pdf}}}[\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t6}}+\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t7}}]=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bc.pdf}}]\,,\\
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bb.pdf}}}[\Psi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t1}}]&=\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bb.pdf}}}[\phi_{\includegraphics[]{figures/amp_t10}}]=\Omega[\mathcal{Z}_{\includegraphics[scale,align]{figures/delta_bb.pdf}}]\,.\end{aligned}

(121)

The zonotopes of the 3-site chain are presented in figure
9
(
red
).
Further residues of the physical FRW-form are governed by the combinatorics of these zonotopes leading the the flow of (physical) cuts in figure
9
.

Figure 9:
Flow of cuts for the 3-site chain graph.
The top-dimensional facets of the zonotope are
red
, the codimension-1 facets are
cyan
and the codimension-2 facets are
magenta
.

5
Deriving the kinematic flow

In section
5.1
, we derive the differential equations for the cut basis, without input from bulk physics, using intersection theory and simple residue calculus.
Since the flow of physical sequential residues/cuts is tied to the combinatorics of zonotopes (c.f., section
4
), the differential equations inherit the
reverse
of this flow.
18
18

18

The flow is reversed for the differential equations because discontinuities (computed by changing the contour to one that has a residue component) and the differential are correlated by the coproduct but act on opposite ends of the tensor product
Abreu:2019wzk
;
McLeodPokrakaRen
.
Another way to see this reversal is by noting that the differential equations of the FRW-cohomology and dual-cohomology are related my minus transpose
Caron-Huot:2021xqj
;
De:2023xue
and because the coboundary symbols
\delta_{\bullet}
are directly related to sequential residues.

In section
5.2
, we provide worked examples for the master formula for kinematic flow.

Mathematica
code for many of the worked examples and more (such as the five-site chain and the two-loop/cycle kite) can be found at the github repository

or in the ancillary files.

5.1
The differential equations from intersection theory

In this section, we sketch the intersection theory computation for the DEQs.
We provide the basis of forms dual to the those introduced in section
3.3
.
Then using these dual forms, we provide a residue formula for the matrix elements of the DEQ.
More details can be found in appendix
B
.

The basis of forms dual to
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
is

\displaystyle\check{\phi}_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}\,\delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\left(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\delta_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\text{sgn}_{\tau}\delta_{\tau}\,,

(122)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}=\bigwedge_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}

where we recall that inside the intersection number
\delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\to\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Furthermore, the argument of
\delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
must be a form restricted to the cut
M_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
In fact, we use the canonical form,
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}=\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
, of the cut geometry,
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
, as the argument for the
\delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
19
19

19

Note that we use
|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
to denote the pull-back to the cut associated this cut.

These dual forms are designed to have a diagonal intersection matrix with the cut basis
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Note that in the wedge product,
\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}
comes before
\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{2}}
if
\min(V_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}})<\min(V_{\mathfrak{l}_{2}})
.

Assuming that the FRW-form
\phi
has only simple poles, the intersection number has a simple localization formula

\displaystyle\langle\check{\phi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\phi\rangle=\langle\delta_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}(\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}})|\phi\rangle

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}\overset{\text{Res}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}}}{\overbrace{\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\circ\cdots\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}}}\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}[\phi]

(123)

where
\text{Res}_{\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}}
is the (leading order term of the) residue operator generated by the canonical form
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
and each

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{1}{\prod_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{v}}\right)\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\mathrm{sig}(v,V_{\mathfrak{l}}\setminus v)\alpha_{v}\underset{u\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}\setminus v}{\bigcirc}\text{Res}_{x_{v}=0}

(124)

is the (leading order term of the) residue operator generated by the factor
\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}
in the product (
95
).
The residue operator
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}
is a weighted sum of residues that localizes to the vertices of the simplex whose canonical form is
\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Note that we order the
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}
such that
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}
comes before
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{2}}
if
\min(V_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}})<\min(V_{\mathfrak{l}_{2}})
.

Using (
123
), (
124
), and (
97
), computing the intersection matrix is straightforward

\displaystyle C_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}:=\langle\check{\phi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\phi_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\rangle=|\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\delta_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\frac{\sum_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{l}}{\prod_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{l}}\,.

(125)

This matrix is diagonal since the residue operator
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
in (
123
) projects out a single element of our basis
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}[\phi_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}]=|\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\delta_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then, each
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}
act on each
\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}
that makes up
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Each
\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}
is the canonical form of a simplex in the product decomposition of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
See appendix
B.1
for a simple derivation of (
125
).

Having a diagonal intersection matrix (
125
) makes it easy to derive the differential equations from (
62
).
Moreover, since we have designed our basis of FRW- and dual-forms to have only one independent cut the resulting DEQs will have the minimal amount of mixing between different cuts.

To compute the differential equations, we project
\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
back onto the cut basis.
However,
\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
has double poles and the simplified formulas for the intersection number ((
123
), (
124
), and (
97
)) cannot be used.
To remedy this, we need a representation for
\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
with only simple poles.

To find a form cohomologous (differs by a total covariant derivative) to
\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
with simple poles, it is useful to promote the exterior derivative on the integration variables to the exterior derivative on the combined integration and kinematic space:

\displaystyle\text{d}\to\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\text{d}x_{v}\,\partial_{x_{v}}+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\text{d}X_{v}\,\partial_{X_{v}}+\sum_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}Y_{e}\,\partial_{Y_{e}}\,,

(126)

(see
(
De:2023xue
, section 3.3)
for more on this technique).
Then, any logarithmic differential form (all forms so far have been logarithmic differential forms: the FRW-forms, dual-forms and the connection
\omega:=\mathrm{dlog}u=\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\alpha_{v}\mathrm{dlog}x_{v}
) can be
canonically
upgraded to forms on the larger integration plus kinematic space by interpreting
d
as in (
126
).
The total covariant derivative of the upgraded FRW-forms
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
is especially simple and leads to

\displaystyle\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\simeq\left(\omega\wedge\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\right)_{\text{d}\text{ as in \eqref{eq:dtot}}}|_{\cdots\wedge\text{d}Z\wedge\cdots\wedge\text{d}Z^{\prime}\wedge\cdots\to 0}\,,

(127)

where
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\{X_{v},Y_{e}\}
and
\simeq
denotes equivalence in twisted cohomology in the usual sense (using
\nabla
with
d
acting only on the integration variables).
Equation (
127
) allows us to use the simplified formulas for the intersection number: (
123
), (
124
), and (
97
).

For the remainder of this derivation we will interpret
d
as in (
126
) and extract the appropriate component of the differential form by sending
\cdots\wedge\text{d}Z\wedge\cdots\wedge\text{d}Z^{\prime}\wedge\cdots\to 0
.
Moreover, note that the residue operators of the intersection number project out the appropriate component of the differential form
\omega\wedge\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
interpreted on the integration plus kinematic space automatically.
Therefore, we leave this projection implicit.

Using (
123
), the diagonal entries of the DEQ are simple to evaluate

\displaystyle A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\langle\check{\phi}_{\mathfrak{g}}|\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\rangle}\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\left[\omega\wedge\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}\right]\,,

(128)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|V_{G}|}\sum_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\bigg(\sum_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{l}\bigg)\mathrm{dlog}f_{\mathfrak{l}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\,.

To pass from the first to second line in (
128
), we move
\omega
so that it appears after
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}
at the cost of a sign.
Then,
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
is easy to perform.
After this residue, the remaining differential form is simply
(-1)^{|V_{G}|}\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\omega|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Next, we perform the residues
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}
by breaking
\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\omega|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
into its constituent pieces by isolating the
x_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}
-dependence

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\omega|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}(-1)^{\bullet}\;\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\left(\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}\wedge\omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}\right)\wedge\cdots\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}

(129)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathfrak{l}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{l}\;\mathrm{dlog}x_{l}\bigg|_{\sum_{i\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}x_{i}+f_{\mathfrak{l}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=0}

where the exact form of the sign
(-1)^{\bullet}
is not important for understanding gross features of the localization in (
128
).
Since
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}]\propto\delta_{\mathfrak{l}\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\left[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\left(\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}\wedge\omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}\right)\wedge\cdots\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}\right]\\
&=\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{1}}]\cdots\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}\wedge\omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{i}}]\cdots\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}|}}]\,.\end{aligned}

(130)

The factors
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}]
evaluate to constants while the
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{l}}\wedge\omega_{\mathfrak{l}}]
is proportional to a combination of the
\mathrm{dlog}x_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}
where each
\mathrm{dlog}x_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}
is subject to the conditions
\sum_{i\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}x_{i}+f_{\mathfrak{l}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=0
(from the cut) and
x_{k\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}\setminus\{l\}}=0
(from the residue
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}
).
Fortunately, these conditions have the same solution for each
x_{l\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}
as seen in the second line of (
128
) where there is only one
\mathrm{dlog}
for each letter
\mathfrak{l}
.
A detailed derivation of (
128
) can be found in appendix
B.2
.

Next are the off-diagonal terms

\displaystyle A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{|V_{G}|}}{\langle\check{\phi}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|\phi_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\rangle}\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|}}\circ\cdots\circ\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}\left[\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\omega\right]\,.

(131)

Obviously,
A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\neq 0
if and only if
\text{Res}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}[\mathrm{dlog}\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}]\neq 0
.
This only happens when all tubings in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
can be obtained from a tubing in
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}
by adding additional compatible tubes.
This makes it clear that forms associated to acyclic minors with different broken edges cannot couple in the differential equation since the tubings associated to these acyclic minors necessarily contain an incompatible pair.
Moreover, the residues
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}
localize to where the
x_{i}|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}=0
and are non-trivial if and only if there are enough
\mathrm{dlog}x_{i}
’s in
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}\wedge\omega
.
This further restricts which matrix elements
A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}
are non-trivial.
Indeed,
A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\neq 0
if and only if for all
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}
there exists a tubing
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau
differ by exactly one tube:
|\tau\setminus\tau^{\prime}|=1
.
This is because
\omega
can only contribute a single factor of
\mathrm{dlog}x_{i}
.
Translating this condition into a condition on acyclic minors implies that
A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\neq 0
if and only if the pair
(\mathfrak{g},{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}})
are related by a merger (c.f.,
3.2
).
That is, the differential equations inherit the combinatorics of the zonotopes governing the cuts.

Then, for a pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
that are related by a merger, we have

\displaystyle A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\frac{\left(\sum_{i\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}}\alpha_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{j}\right)}{\sum_{k\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}\cup V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{k}}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{f_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})}{f_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})}\,,

(132)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\phantom{-}1&\text{if oriented edges connecting $\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}$ to $\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}$ point away from $\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}$}\\
-1&\text{if oriented edges connecting $\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}$ to $\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}$ point towards $\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}$}\end{cases}\,,

where we recall that
\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}<\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}
.
Up to the overall sign, the factors in this formula are easy to understand.
After taking the cut,

\displaystyle A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\propto}\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|}{\langle\check{\phi}_{{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}|\phi_{{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}\rangle}\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}_{|\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|}}{\circ}{\cdots}{\circ}\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}_{1}}\left[\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathfrak{g}\setminus\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}{\wedge}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{g}}{\wedge}\omega\right]\bigg|_{B_{t^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}\,,

(133)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathfrak{g}\setminus{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{dlog}B_{t\in\tau\setminus\tau^{\prime}}\bigg|_{B_{t^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}\,\forall\,(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\,,

(134)

where we recall the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}
in equation (
86
).
Then, ignoring the overall sign, the residue in
A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}
factorizes since each
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}|_{B_{t\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}
depends solely on the
x_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}

\displaystyle A_{\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\propto\underset{\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}}\alpha_{v}}{\sum_{v\in V_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}}\alpha_{v}}=\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}\cup V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{v}}{\sum_{vV_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}\cup V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{v}}}{\underbrace{\frac{|\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|}{\langle\check{\phi}_{{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}|\phi_{{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}\rangle}\left(\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}\cup\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}}\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}\bigg|_{B_{t\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}\right)}}\text{Res}_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}\left[\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}{\wedge}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}{\wedge}\omega_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}{\wedge}\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathfrak{g}\setminus\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\right]\bigg|_{B_{t^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}\,,

(135)

where
\text{Res}_{\mathfrak{l}}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}}|_{B_{t^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}=\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{v}/\prod_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{v}
,
V_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}=V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}\cup V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}
and the only part of
\omega
that survives the restriction of the wedge product to the cut is
\omega_{\mathfrak{l}}=\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}}\alpha_{v}\mathrm{dlog}x_{v}+\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{v}\mathrm{dlog}x_{v}
.
To get (
132
) up to the overall sign, use

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}\left[\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}{\wedge}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}{\wedge}\omega_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}}{\wedge}\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathfrak{g}\setminus\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}\right]\bigg|_{B_{t^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime}}=0}\propto\frac{\left(\sum_{i\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}}\alpha_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{j}\right)}{\prod_{k\in V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}\cup V_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}}\alpha_{k}}\mathrm{dlog}\frac{f_{\mathfrak{l}_{\uparrow}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})}{f_{\mathfrak{l}_{\downarrow}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})}

(136)

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathfrak{g}\setminus\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|_{x_{i\in V_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\uparrow}}}=0}

\displaystyle=f_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\uparrow}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dlog}B_{\mathfrak{g}\setminus\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}|_{x_{i\in V_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\downarrow}}}=0}

\displaystyle=f_{\underline{\mathfrak{l}}_{\downarrow}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\,.

(137)

Getting the sign correct takes careful bookkeeping; a full derivation can be found in appendix
B.3
.

5.2
Examples

To summarize the results of the previous section, the action of the derivative on an arbitrary element of the cut basis is given by

\displaystyle(-1)^{|V_{\mathfrak{g}}|}\ \text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}}\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{v}\ \mathrm{dlog}\left(f_{\mathfrak{l}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathfrak{l}\rightarrow\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}\frac{\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{v}\sum_{v^{\prime}\in V_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}}{\sum_{v\in V_{\mathfrak{l}}}\alpha_{v}+\sum_{v^{\prime}\in V_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}}\alpha_{v^{\prime}}}\mathrm{dlog}\left(\frac{f_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}}{f_{\mathfrak{l}}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{l}\cup\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}}.

(138)

The sum on the second line is over all pairs of letters
\{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}\}\subset\mathbb{L}_{\mathfrak{g}}
for which there exists at least one oriented edge from
V_{\mathfrak{l}}
to
V_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, we use
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{l}\cup\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
to denote the acyclic minor obtained from
\mathfrak{g}
by replacing all oriented edges between
V_{\mathfrak{l}}
and
V_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
with solid edges.

As emphasized throughout the paper, there are two important consequences which follow from the fact that only acyclic minors related by a merger—defined as the exchange of oriented edges with solid edges—can couple to one another in the differential equations. First, the differential equations decouple into
2^{|E_{G}|}
blocks, one for each choice of broken edges. Second, within each block, the acyclic minors with fixed broken edges label the boundary stratification of the graphical zonotopes introduced in section
4
. As a result, the differential equations inherit this zonotopal structure as we now demonstrate through examples.

Throughout the examples we abuse notation and use a letter
\mathfrak{l}
to denote
\mathrm{dlog}(f_{\mathfrak{l}})
.
For example, in the context of the three-site chain

\displaystyle\raisebox{1.42271pt}{\hbox to26.28pt{\vbox to3.84pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.92pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

and the three-cycle

\displaystyle\raisebox{-6.544pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_line1_let1}}\equiv\mathrm{dlog}(X_{1}+X_{3}-Y_{12}-Y_{23}),

\displaystyle\raisebox{-6.544pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_bulk_let}}\equiv\mathrm{dlog}(X_{1}+X_{2}+X_{3}).

(140)

We also do not exemplify the differential equation for every element of the cut basis in each example since the cut basis is generally too large to fit into the main text of this work.
However, the interested reader can find a the full differential equation for the one-loop bubble or two-cycle graph in appendix
A
.
The full results of the worked examples and more can be found at the github repository

or in the ancillary files.

5.2.1
Three-site chain

10
. First, the point on the left-hand-side of figure

(143)

On the right-hand side two new functions appear, each corresponding to one of the codimension-one faces which contain the specified vertex in their boundary. Next, consider the derivative acting on the first new function appearing in (

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 0 M 33.71 0 C 33.71 1.22 32.72 2.21 31.5 2.21 C 30.27 2.21 29.28 1.22 29.28 0 C 29.28 -1.22 30.27 -2.21 31.5 -2.21 C 32.72 -2.21 33.71 -1.22 33.71 0 Z M 31.5 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(144)

In this last step no new elements of the cut basis are generated and the kinematic flow for this sector terminates.

Figure 10:
The differential equations for the path graph on three vertices decomposes into four blocks each described by the boundary stratification of a graphical zonotope.

5.2.2
Three-cycle

The first seven blocks of the kinematic flow for the three-cycle exhibit a flow structure analogous to those found for the three-site chain, with graphical zonotopes corresponding to points, line segments and squares, as demonstrated in figure
11
. The final block, however, introduces a novel
(13\times 13)
system of differential equations, whose kinematic flow is characterized by the hexagon shown in Figure
11
. For example, consider the right most vertex of the hexagon, its differential is given by

\displaystyle-\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert.pdf}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\alpha_{1}\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_1.pdf}}+\alpha_{2}\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_2.pdf}}+\alpha_{3}\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_3.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert.pdf}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}}\left(\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_3.pdf}}-\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_1.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_line_1.pdf}}+\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\left(\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_1.pdf}}-\raisebox{-8.5359pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_let_2.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_line_2.pdf}}.

(145)

As for the three-site chain, two new terms have been generated corresponding to the two line segments which intersect at the chosen vertex. Applying the derivative to the first element of the cut basis on the second line we find

\displaystyle{-}\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_line_1.pdf}}\simeq\left((\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{3})\raisebox{-6.544pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_line1_let1}}+\alpha_{2}\raisebox{-6.544pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_line1_let2}}\right){\wedge}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_vert_line_1.pdf}}{+}\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3})\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}\left(\raisebox{-6.544pt}{
\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_line1_let1}
}{-}\raisebox{-6.544pt}{
\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_line1_let2}
}\right){\wedge}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_bulk.pdf}}.

(146)

Again, a single new function is generated, associated to the bulk of the hexagon. The kinematic flow for this block terminates with

\displaystyle-\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_bulk.pdf}}\simeq\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}\right)\raisebox{-6.544pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_bulk_let.pdf}}\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/c3_bulk.pdf}}.

(147)

Figure 11:
A subset of the graphical zonotopes which describe the kinematic flow for the three-cycle.

5.2.3
Four-cycle

All but one of the blocks in the kinematic flow for the four-cycle are described by zonotopes given by points, line segments, squares and cubes. The structure of the final block is captured by the graphical zonotope displayed in figure
12
. For illustration, consider the function associated with the top front vertex of the zonotope, we have

\displaystyle\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\alpha_{1}\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_1.pdf}}+\alpha_{2}\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_2.pdf}}+\alpha_{3}\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_3.pdf}}+\alpha_{4}\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_4.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4}}\left(\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_4.pdf}}-\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_1.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d}}+\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\left(\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_2.pdf}}-\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_1.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_l}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}\left(\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_3.pdf}}-\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_2.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_u}}+\frac{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}}{\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}}\left(\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_3.pdf}}-\raisebox{-7.11317pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_let_4.pdf}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_r}}.

(148)

The appearance of four new functions on the right-hand side reflects the fact that the corresponding vertex of the graphical zonotope is non-simple, lying at the intersection of four line segments. The derivative of the first function on the second line yields

\displaystyle\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4}\right)\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_1}}+\alpha_{2}\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_2}}+\alpha_{3}\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_3}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{4})\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{4}}\left(\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_2}}-\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_1}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l}}+\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}\left(\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_3}}-\raisebox{-4.83691pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_let_2}}\right)\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_u}}.

(149)

In this case we generate two new functions corresponding to the two codimension-one faces intersecting on this edge. Applying the derivative to the first results in the following

\displaystyle\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left((\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{4})\raisebox{-5.12128pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l_let_1}}{+}\alpha_{3}\raisebox{-5.12128pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l_let_2}}\right){\wedge}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l}}{+}\frac{(\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{4})\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{1}{+}\alpha_{2}{+}\alpha_{3}{+}\alpha_{4}}\left(\raisebox{-5.12128pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l_let_2}}{-}\raisebox{-5.12128pt}{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_14_d_l_let_1}}\right){\wedge}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_double}}.

(150)

This function corresponds to a codimension-one face of the graphical zonotope, therefore, its derivative generates only one new function, corresponding to the bulk of the polytope. Finally, applying the derivative to this last function produces no further terms, and the kinematic flow terminates with

\displaystyle\text{d}_{\mathrm{kin}}\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_double}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{4}\right)\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{ \includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_double_red}}\wedge\phi_{\includegraphics[scale]{figures/cycle4_double}}.

(151)

Figure 12:
The graphical zonotope (rhombic dodecahedron) describing the kinematic flow for a block in the differential equations for the four-cycle.

6
Conclusion

It was recently proposed that the independent cuts of the physical FRW-form naturally define a set of forms known as the
cut basis

De:2024zic
. Building on this insight, we have shown that the cut basis and its associated
kinematic flow
are governed by rich combinatorial structures. Each element of the cut basis corresponds one-to-one with a
positive geometry
associated with a cut of the physical FRW-from. To systematically label elements of this basis, we introduced the notion of an
acyclic minor
and provided a simple prescription for assigning to each a
logarithmic
differential form. With the cut basis at hand, we employed the framework of relative twisted cohomology and intersection theory to derive the corresponding system of differential equations. Crucially, at no point in our analysis have we relied on input from bulk physics to derive these equations.

We provide a number of explicit examples of the master formula (
138
) in the text.
Additionally,
Mathematica
code for these and other examples (such as the five-site chain and the two-loop/cycle kite) can be found at the github repository

or in the ancillary files.

A remarkable feature of our differential equations is that only acyclic minors related by a
merger
couple to one another. As a consequence, the kinematic flow is naturally organized into exponentially many sectors, each with the structure of a graphical zonotope. While this zonotopal organization had been observed previously
Baumann:2025qjx
, it was interpreted merely as a combinatorial artifact of the differential equations. In contrast, we uncover its true geometric origin: the zonotopes reside within the cosmological hyperplane arrangement, and their canonical forms compute residues of the physical FRW-form. Therefore, these zonotopes not only reflect the geometry underlying the differential equations but also govern the cut combinatorics—
flow of cuts
—of both the physical FRW-form and the cut basis itself. Thus, the combinatorics of the kinematic flow emerge as a natural consequence of the geometric and combinatorial structure of the flow of cuts.

At the level of the symbol, the kinematic flow controls which letters can be added to the end of the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\bullet}^{k})
component of the symbol to generate the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\bullet}^{k+1})
component of the symbol for a physical FRW integral in the
\alpha_{\bullet}\to 0
expansion.
On the other hand, the flow of cuts controls the sequential discontinuities of the physical FRW integrals.
At the level of the symbol, a discontinuity annihilates all terms that do not have the compatible letter as their first entry.
For terms with the compatible letter, the discontinuity simply removes the first entry.
With these two handles on the analytic structure of FRW integrals, it would be interesting to look for symmetries (such as antipoldal duality) in the symbols of FRW integrals.
Perhaps it is even possible to assign a cluster(-like) structure to the symbol.

The differential equations derived here are relevant for a single Feynman graph contribution to the wavefunction coefficient. It would be interesting to consider differential equations for the wavefunction as a whole and to make connection to recently discovered geometries
Arkani-Hamed:2024jbp
;
Glew:2025otn
;
Forcey:2025voc
.

It would also be interesting to understand how to the story changes when the scalar field has a generic mass (i.e., not conformally coupled). Do zonotopes play an important role there too?
Could the cut-first approach help make an invariant definition of the physical subspace in this case?
Which combinatorial parts of the differential equations survive after loop integration, if any?
We leave these any many other interesting questions for future research.
